## Page 140


The advantage of the coordinates (τ, ˆφi) (or (τ, φi), (t, ϕi) etc.) over ψj coordinates is that they better
demonstrate the global symmetries of the spacetime. In the presence of NUT parameters, things can get more
complicated, but in the case of Kerr–AdS (or background AdS), or even a generalized Kerr–AdS where X is
a general function of r but the other Xα = ¯Xα = Y (yα), each of ˆφi, φi, ϕi are canonically normalized to have
period 2π, such that the points (τ, ˆφ1, . . . ˆφi, . . . , ˆφn−1+ε, r, yα) and (τ, ˆφ1, . . . , ˆφi +2π, . . . , ˆφn−1+ε, r, yα) are
identified (and similarly for φi, ϕi). By contrast, the ψj’s periodicities are functions of the ai in a complicated
way (and must basically be constructed from the periodicities of the ˆφi coordinates). On the other hand, the
ψj coordinates simplify the metric expressions and as we will see they also represent important symmetries
in the spacetime. β = ∂/∂ψ0 in particular will turn out to be very important.
Note that since the ψj are linearly related to τ, ˆφi, all of the ∂/∂ψj are Killing vectors. (This is also
apparent from the fact that the basis one-forms, and thus the metric, do not depend on ψj.)

I will say a quick word on dimensions (in the sense of, “dimensions of length”). As usual let c = G =
kB = 1, but for the moment relax the ℏ= 1 condition. Then the only dimension of interest is length,
which I designate L. ds2 has dimensions of length squared or L2. That means that each eA has dimensions
of L1.
r, xµ, ai, l all have dimensions of L1, and Qµ all have dimensions L0.

into discussing the Kerr–anti-de Sitter spacetime and the Kerr–Schild form.
3.3
Generalized Kerr–anti-de Sitter: Kerr–Schild Form and Black Hole Horizons
We return to the GKAdS spacetimes, which are specifically those for which ¯Xα = Y (yα). There are really
two spacetimes involved, a background pure AdS spacetime for which Xn = −¯X = Y (xn) = Y (ir), and a
full spacetime where Xn = −X, an arbitrary function of r, satisfying
X = ¯X −2µ(r)r1−ε,
(3.3.1)
106


## Page 150


where µ(r) = m is the Kerr–AdS solution. Of course, both the full and background spacetimes are GKAdS
and thus also part of the Generalized Kerr–NUT–AdS spacetime, and so both have the associated symmetries.
Consider now the Kerr–Schild null vector k. Chen and L¨u [27] demonstrated that the Kerr–NUT–AdS
spacetimes can be written in multi-Kerr–Schild form, including the specific case of the Kerr–AdS spacetime.
In [48], it is made explicit that the Kerr–Schild null vectors are the WANDs of the spacetime (that is, parallel
to the mµ or m∗
µ). I use slightly different notation but my argument is essentially the same, though I will
go through the derivations because I use some of the results (in my notation) going forward.

While we can refer to h associated with the background AdS spacetime as a PCKY tensor, it is worth
116


## Page 160


noting that it is not the unique PCKY tensor of the AdS background, as there are a large number of distinct
Closed Conformal Killing–Yano tensors for pure AdS. For our purposes all that is required to see this is
to note that a different set of choices for the ai terms would lead to a different initial set of spheroidal
coordinates and consequently a different set of Jacobi transformed coordinates, and thus a different PCKY
tensor, which would not be equal to h even up to coordinate transformations. Another way to say this is
that the AdS spacetime has such a high degree of symmetry that it has an infinite array of PCKY tensors.
I will say more about this in Section 3.4. Let it be understood that when I discuss h or β associated with
the background AdS spacetime, it is the h, β defined above: the ones that are equal to the h, β in the full
spacetime which is related to the background spacetime by a Kerr–Schild relationship.
Generalized Kerr–NUT–AdS as Multiple Kerr–Schild Perturbation
Chen and L¨u [27] demonstrate that the Kerr–NUT–AdS spacetime can be written in multiple Kerr–Schild
form, just as Kerr–AdS can be written in Kerr–Schild form (see also [48]). The disadvantage of the multiple
Kerr–Schild form is that it requires a Wick rotation of some of the coordinates, so that some of the coordinates
are now imaginary.
The argument is as follows. The result from Section 3.3.1 is that it is possible to take an AdS spacetime
written in terms of canonical basis vectors ¯eA and use one of the eigenvectors of the associated PCKY
tensor h to generate a GKAdS spacetime, with (in certain senses) the same h. The eigenvector we chose, k,
involved only the ¯en, ¯eˆn basis one-forms. We can easily generalize the process to involve other eigenvectors
of h—creating a multiple Kerr–Schild form, as follows. Requiring ¯Xµ = Y (xµ) and letting Xµ(xµ) be, in

This tells us that we can write any member of the GKNAdS (note the N) class as a multiple Kerr–Schild
perturbation to a pure AdS spacetime, with the significant caveat that, if the ψj are real, in general the ¯ψj
will not be real. It is unclear whether there is any benefit to this decomposition. The reason that the GKAdS
(no N) are special is because the fact that xn, and only xn, is imaginary in those spacetimes means that the
vector k = ˜mn associated with the xn eigenvalue is real-valued whereas the other ˜mµ are not. While my
work focuses on the non-NUT case, I mention this here because (as I mention in the Future Work section) it
may be possible to apply some of the results associated with the GKAdS spacetimes to the GKNAdS ones,
using the multiple-Kerr–Schild form.
Note again nomenclature: what I call the Generalized Kerr–NUT–AdS is referred to in the literature as
the “off-shell Kerr–NUT–(A)dS” in, e.g., [48].
3.3.2
Black Hole Horizon
The Kerr–AdS spacetimes are stationary black holes, with an event horizon located where V = 2m, as
discussed in [51, 52]. Their results generalize readily to the GKAdS spacetimes where m →µ(r). In either
form of the Boyer–Lindquist coordinates, grr diverges when V = 2µ(r), which also corresponds to X = 0.
This is the horizon location. In general there are multiple solutions to V = 2µ(r). Let the largest solution of
V = 2µ(r) be r = r+. If r+ is real and positive, then we will consider this the location of the event horizon,
H, of the Generalized Kerr–AdS black hole. In four dimensions, this is the solution to
(r2
+ + a2)(1 + r2
+l−2) −2µ(r)r+ = 0.
(3.3.47)
We will be focused on the outer horizon (r+) rather than inner horizons (labelled something like r−). Because
we are only concerned with one horizon I will just write H instead of H+ (the + label on r+ is to distinguish
it from the coordinate r in general).
Because the horizon is located at constant r, grr = 0 (on H, or any other horizon). Using the Kerr–Schild
coordinates and decomposition, grr = ¯grr −Hkrkr = ¯grr −H (since kr = 1), so the horizon can also be
found where H = ¯grr = ¯g−1
rr (which is consistent with V = 2µ(r)).
Because the Generalized Kerr–AdS black holes are stationary, the event horizon is also a Killing horizon,
where a Killing vector field becomes null. I will call this Killing vector field, normalized so that its t or τ

3.3.3
Ring Singularity
As pointed out by CGKP [34] (and possibly earlier), the ring singularity in the Kerr–AdS spacetime is
located at r = 0 in even dimension, but r2 + a2
n = 0 (where an is the smallest of the ai parameters) in odd
dimension. As a result, when discussing the region below the event horizon, in the KS coordinates which
extend below said horizon, we will always be discussing the region r > 0 for D even and r2 > −a2
n for D
odd.
This means that r actually takes on imaginary values if D is odd; we really require r2 > −a2
n rather than
the more usual r2 > 0. In that sense, r2 might be a better coordinate than r to use, at least for small values
of r2, but for our purposes it is sufficient to keep this in mind.
Note that this is consistent with the spheroidal transformation (3.1.15). In even dimension, a2
n = 0, and

so the equation y2ˆµ2
n = r2µ2
n implies that r = 0 corresponds to y = 0 (except possibly if ˆµn = 0). In odd
dimensions, r = 0 simply gives
y2ˆµ2
i = a2
i µ2
i
Ξi
,
(3.3.67)
which does not in general imply y = 0. It requires r2 + a2
i = 0 to give y = 0, and so r2 extends down to −a2
n
in the spheroidal coordinate transformation.
3.4
Pseudo-Cartesian Coordinates for Generalized Kerr–anti-de Sitter
In this section I introduce a set of pseudo-Cartesian coordinates for Generalized Kerr–anti-de Sitter in its
Kerr–Schild form, which I believe to be novel (including for Kerr–anti-de Sitter). These are based on a
well-known set of coordinates describing anti-de Sitter.
It is known that it is possible to embed D-dimensional anti-de Sitter into a flat space of dimension one
higher with signature (D −3), or, specifically, with signature (−−+ . . . +) (see, for instance, [123]; see also
[71] which considers the O(3, 2) charges associated with asymptotically anti-de Sitter spacetimes). Let AdS
space be expressed locally in terms of coordinates (u, v, xi, yi, z) for even dimensions and (u, v, xi, yi) for odd
dimensions. Here 1 ≤i ≤n −1 + ε. I will call these coordinates pseudo-Cartesian. The metric is
d¯s2 = −du2 −dv2 +
n−1+ε

u =

It is understood (here and for the rest of Section 3.4) that the z coordinate transformation is only meaningful
if z exists (if D is even). (Note that the yi coordinates represent coordinates associated with the 2-plane
with associated coordinates µi, ˆµi, ϕi etc. and that y represents the spherical polar radius. yi is also not the
same as the yα associated with the Jacobi transformations. Similarly, the xi are not to be confused with the
xµ.)
To get to an AdS which does not have closed timelike curves, AdS space must be “unwound” from the
embedding I have just described, in the sense that the (D + 1)-dimensional flat space with coordinates
(u, v, xi, yi, z) has t/l acting as a periodic coordinate, where the points t = t0 and t = t0 + 2πl are identified
in that they lead to the same values of u and v. I consider AdS to be the universal covering space, where
t varies from −∞to ∞(without the periodic identification above). The unwinding issue is a disadvantage
of this embedding into (D + 1)-dimensional flat coordinates, which are not valid for describing the global
spacetime.

Interestingly, kaka (without applying the constraint) is then simply equal to the left-hand side of (3.4.5).
This tells us that the statement that kaka is null in the pseudo-Cartesian coordinates is essentially the same
condition as the implicit definition of r.
The immediate interpretation here is that ka∂a is the Kerr–Schild null vector in the D-dimensional anti-de
Sitter space, with (D+1) coordinates and also the constraint (3.4.2). It is also worth checking what happens
if we consider ka∂a, with the same coordinates, in the (D + 1)-dimensional flat space with no constraint.

3.4.2
Embedding in Higher Dimensions
Interestingly, there is a natural embedding of these GKAdS solutions in higher dimensions, which is achieved
by taking the for GKAdS as described earlier in this section, using the Kerr–Schild form ds2 = d¯s2 +
2µ(r)
U
(kadxa) with background metric (3.4.1), U as given by (3.4.19) and ka as given by (3.4.8). The only
change required to make this an embedding in a higher dimension is to remove the constraint (3.4.2). The
resultant metric is (D + 1)-dimensional with (non-Lorentzian) signature (D −3). It is not expected to have
physical significance, but is mathematically interesting and may yield more insights about the structure of
the Kerr–AdS metrics. Kerr–AdS is recovered by applying the constraint.
The form that the metric takes is again very similar to the (Kerr–Schild) form of the Myers–Perry metrics
in Cartesian coordinates. It also remarkably has the property that, if µ(r) = m, the background metric is
completely flat and that the full metric is Ricci-flat: Rab = 0. Because the embedding is not used in the rest
of this work, I will place this in Appendix C.
3.4.3
Note on Kerr–de Sitter
This brief note does not appear in the thesis.
Before continuing, I will note that while my focus has been on Kerr–AdS, the Kerr–de Sitter (Kerr–dS)
case is mathematically very similar, and essentially results from sending l →il in the metric, so that l
becomes the de Sitter radius of curvature instead of the AdS radius of curvature. The pseudo-Cartesian
coordinates associated with Kerr–dS can be found by taking the Kerr–AdS pseudo-Cartesian coordinates

## Page 182


Chapter 4
The Covariant Phase Space Formalism and the
Introduction of a Conserved Charge for
Kerr–Schild Spacetimes
In this chapter, I use the covariant phase space methods of Wald and others, often referred to as the Iyer–
Wald formalism from [81], as well as a particular method by Barnich and Comp`ere [13] involving integration
through solution space (the part of field configuration space corresponding to solutions to a given theory), to
define a conserved charge associated with a Kerr–Schild spacetime. This conserved charge can be calculated
by an integration over an arbitrary (D −2)-surface, and is related both to conserved quantities and to
the variations of quantities on the horizon. It is thus a natural way to approach defining thermodynamic
quantities which should obey the first law of black hole mechanics.
The breakdown of the chapter is as follows. I begin in Section 4.1 by reviewing the results for thermo-
dynamics of Kerr–AdS black holes from [53, 34]. In Section 4.2 I then describe the covariant phase space
formalism, in the manner of various papers by Wald and others, for defining conserved quantities associated
with spacetimes, and the way that these relate to black hole mechanics. The result is that a (D −2)-form
(using notation matching that of [13, 32, 59] and others) kχ[δg; g] is found, associated to each Killing vec-
tor χ, and depending on the metric and its variation between solutions “nearby” in solution space. When
139


## Page 183


kχ[δg; g] is integrated over a suitable (D−2)-surface, it gives the variation in a conserved quantity associated
with χ.
Because the covariant phase space formalism generally involves quantities “at infinity,” I will make use
of some ideas from Barnich and Comp`ere (BC) [13] who develop a method of “integrating through solution
space” in order to define conserved quantities at finite radius, including at a black hole horizon. This allows
for direct evaluation of terms involved in the black hole first law and Smarr relation. The introduction of
the BC method is in Section 4.3 and the application to black hole mechanics is in Section 4.4, with some
additional notes about the Smarr relation in Section 4.5.
In Section 4.6, I adapt BC’s method to Kerr–Schild spacetimes in what I believe to be a novel way. I use
this adaptation to define a conserved charge associated with a Kerr–Schild spacetime at arbitrary radius,
rather than merely at infinity. I make some comments on black hole mechanics and the Smarr relation. I
relate the new conserved charge associated with the Kerr–Schild decomposition to the stress–energy tensor
in Section 4.7. I conclude the chapter with some notes on the connection of the Kerr–Schild charge to the
Killing potential (Section 4.8) and to the KBL superpotential (Section 4.9).
The application of this new Kerr–Schild charge to the Kerr–AdS spacetimes will be the subject of Chapter
6.
4.1
Black Hole Thermodynamics for Kerr–AdS Black Holes
To motivate this chapter, I describe the results from two key papers, the first by Gibbons, Perry and Pope
(2005) [53] (hereafter GPP) and the second by Cvetiˇc, Gibbons, Kubizˇn´ak and Pope (2011) [34], hereafter
CGKP. I discussed the latter in Chapter 2 to an extent but review it in greater detail here.
4.1.1
Gibbons, Perry and Pope (2005)
The goal of GPP was to establish a first law of black hole thermodynamics for KAdS black holes. Specifically,
they wanted to find an expression of the form (slightly modifying notation)
dE = TdS +
X
i
ΩidJi,
(4.1.1)
140


## Page 184


for KAdS black holes (in four or higher dimensions), where E is the black hole’s mass (or energy), T is the
black hole temperature, S is the entropy, and Ωi and Ji are the angular velocity and angular momenta
respectively associated with azimuthal symmetry vectors ηi.
They pointed out that whereas there was
agreement in the literature on expressions for the angular momenta, entropy and temperature of KAdS
black holes, there was some controversy over the value for the mass and angular velocities. Their approach
was based on the idea that the first law of black hole mechanics must be satisfied by physically reasonable
definitions for mass and angular velocity, and so they used the uncontroversial expressions for other terms
in (4.1.1) to integrate the first law and to find the required expressions for E and Ωi. They then compared
these expressions with others in the literature. If the first law is satisfied, then TdS + P
i ΩidJi must be an
exact differential (dE).
In natural units (ℏ= 1), GPP use the standard relation S = 1
4A to relate the entropy to the area of the
black hole outer horizon. The temperature T was identified as κ/2π, where κ is the surface gravity (again of
the outer horizon). GPP gave these values in arbitrary dimension; they are written in this thesis in Section
3.3.2. A and thus S are unambiguous, and κ and thus T are unambiguous given the requirement that ζa,
the Killing vector tangent to the null generators of the horizon, must have a “time component” of 1.
For the angular momenta, they calculated the Komar integrals associated with the normalized azimuthal
symmetry vectors ηi. For a generic Killing vector χ, let the Komar differential form KK

GPP point out that the Ashtekar–Magnon–Das (AMD) definition of energy, using conformal methods,
as defined in [6, 7] corresponds to E, provided E is calculated as the conserved charge associated with the
asymptotically nonrotating vector ξ rather than the asymptotically rotating vector β. The AMD prescription
associates to each asymptotic Killing vector a conserved quantity, which I will write as QC[χ] for a generic
Killing vector χ. Using ξ for the asymptotically nonrotating Killing vector ξ as usual, it turns out
E = QC[ξ]

have opposite signs. An alternate orientation for integration surface for which E = −QC[ξ] and Ji = +QC[ηi]
would also be possible.)

The calculation of the Euclidean action is
somewhat complicated and I return to this point, and how the Euclidean action relates to black hole volume,
in Section 5.4.
GPP emphasize the importance of using the conserved charge associated with ξ, the vector nonrotating
at infinity, rather than β, the one appearing in the BL coordinates. They state, “The moral to be extracted
from the above seems to be that one should always use the angular velocities measured relative to a non-
rotating frame at infinity when discussing the thermodynamics of rotating black holes.” They also state,
“The importance of measuring the angular velocity relative to a non-rotating frame was emphasized by
Caldarelli, Cognola and Klemm [18].” Caldarelli et al. point out that the angular velocity Ω(relative to
the nonrotating frame) is the same as the angular velocity of the rotating Einstein universe at infinity, and
argue “The fact that the angular velocity relevant to Kerr–Newman–AdS black hole thermodynamics turns
out to be that of the rotating Einstein universe at the AdS boundary, agrees nicely with the AdS/CFT
correspondence: if the KNAdS black hole in the bulk is described by a conformal field theory living on the
boundary, then the relevant angular velocity entering the thermodynamics should be that of the rotating
Einstein universe at infinity.”
Part of this thesis is my attempt to provide another explanation for why it is so important to use the
asymptotically nonrotating vector in order to have a first law be satisfied. I address it directly in Section
6.2.
GPP point out that the values of E and J for KAdS black holes are, in four dimensions, those found
by Henneaux and Teitelboim [71] consistent with the SO(3, 2) symmetry of AdS, Abbott and Deser [1], and
Caldarelli, Cognola and Klemm [18], whereas the energy reported by Hawking, Hunter and Taylor-Robinson
[65] and Silva [119], labelled E′ by GPP, is given as

CGKP modify the first law as it appears in GPP by also allowing ˜Λ to vary between different KAdS solutions.

Commentary on Komar Integrals and Killing Potentials
Before continuing I want to make a few notes about Komar integrals and the Killing potential method, which

Commentary: “Open Questions” Inspired By GPP and CGKP
Here I make some comments on these papers to motivate my approach for the rest of this thesis.
CGKP show that the conserved charge E associated with ξ is the one which satisfies the first law of black
hole mechanics, whereas the conserved charge F associated with β does not. However, not only does F have
a simpler form than E, but the geometric volume Vgeo appears in the Smarr relation associated with F, rather
than the one associated with E. The geometric volume also satisfies the simple volume–area relationship
Vgeo = r+A/(D−1), and appears naturally in the Killing potential construction as (up to a numerical factor)
the integral of
H
H ∗ωζ, where ωζ is the Killing potential constructed in the unique manner from h (and b′′
in odd dimensions). This all suggests that both ξ and β (E and F) play some sort of important role.
Vgeo is, again as pointed out in [10], also equal to the vector volume associated with either ξ or β,
Vgeo = Vξ,B = Vβ,B
=
Z
Σ
ξadΣa =
Z
Σ
βadΣa,
(4.1.69)
where Σ is a hypersurface extending from r = 0 (even dimension) or r2 + a2
n = 0 (odd dimension) up to the
horizon r = r+. Thus Vgeo = Vξ,B is closely connected with our work.
This raises the following questions, which I address in the remainder of this thesis:
1. Why does the first law work with ξ but not β? (Or: is there more we can say about why it is necessary
158


## Page 202


to use an asymptotically nonrotating frame?)
2. Why is it that the geometric volume appears in the Smarr relation associated with β, but not the one
associated with ξ?
3. Why does the Killing potential method, constructing ω(j) purely out of h (and b′′ for ω(n) in odd
dimensions), produce
H
H ∗ωζ = −2Vgeo?
4. What is the reason for the simple relationship Vgeo = r+A/(D −1) between the geometric volume and
the horizon area?
In this chapter I will use the covariant phase space methods of Wald, along with some specific methods of
Barnich and Comp`ere, to develop a definition of conserved charge which applies to Kerr–Schild spacetimes
such as Kerr–AdS. This gives a conserved charge associated with each Killing vector which is expressible in
terms of Komar integrals and the Kerr–Schild correction to the spacetime, and which can be evaluated on
any surface (not just at infinity).
This charge is constructed to satisfy the first law, but there are some subtleties about why it is that this
charge will satisfy the first law associated with ξ and not with β. A discussion of the first question above,
why the charge associated with ξ satisfies the first law and that associated with β does not, appears later
on, in Section 6.2. Only the D = 4 case is treated directly but I believe the results generalize naturally to
higher dimensions.
The second question, having to do with why the vector volume appears more naturally in the Smarr
relation associated with β rather than the one associated with ξ, is addressed in Section 6.5.
For the first two questions, various approaches have been taken in the literature. For the first question,
authors such as [59, 31, 15, 85] identify the breakdown of the first-law when using a frame adapted to β
rather than ξ as coming down to the non-integrability of the Hamiltonian associated with β. I return to this
point. My work is related to this, though the approach is distinctive.
For the second question, there has been a very recent interest in the difference between the thermodynamic
and geometric volumes, which has appeared in papers such as [134, 135, 20, 21, 22]. Xiao et al. [134, 135]
consider the extended Iyer–Wald formalism, related to the covariant phase space formalism I describe below,
but extended to allow variations in δΛ, and identify the difference between the geometric and thermodynamic
volumes through this method in [134] and, in [135], providing a direct definition for the thermodynamic
159


## Page 203


volume through this method. In [134], they additionally identify the geometric volume as naturally arising
in the Smarr relation due to the difference between integrals of the form
R
ξ · ϵ (in other words, vector
volumes) in the full and background spacetime, and additionally relate it to the difference between the
Komar integral associated with ξ for the full and background spacetime, in a way that is somewhat similar
to my approach below. Campos et al. [20, 21, 22] note that the difference between the geometric/vector
volume and the “potential volume” or thermodynamic volume (associated with the co-potential given above)
is given by the integral of a gauge term. My investigation proceeds along different lines. Additionally, to
my knowledge, a close investigation of why it is that the geometric volume appears in the Smarr relation
associated with F (the conserved quantity associated with β), specifically, has not since been done.
Related to this, there is intriguing work by Gao et al. [49] and Campos et al. [20, 21, 22] (see also [31]
for the Kerr–de Sitter case), in which a variation law (in four dimensions) based not on ξ or β but
1

The third and fourth questions do not require the new charge construction. These can be traced back to
the properties of h and of black hole horizons. These appear as, respectively, Sections 5.5 and 5.6.
I will make an important point about the AMD energy and angular momentum.
In [103], Magnon
argues that the AMD definition is equivalent, up to a possible numerical factor, to the Komar integral for
angular momentum, and, for the mass, to a Komar integral with a background subtraction. In repeating the
calculation, whereas I reproduce her results for the angular momentum, I find that the AMD energy does
not correspond to the value found by Komar subtraction method for ξ, although the AMD and Komar-with-
background-subtraction methods agree if β is used.
4.2
The Covariant Phase Space Formalism
In this section I review some important points of the Covariant Phase Space formalism for the definition
of conserved quantities. The explanation and notation here mostly follows the readable review article [118]
(and references therein), which itself is largely based on several papers by Wald and others, such as [130, 50,
81, 128, 82, 129]. Whereas these papers describe asymptotically flat spacetimes, the covariant phase space
approach generalizes to asymptotically anti-de Sitter spacetimes, as described in, for instance, [73, 8, 59].
My notation more closely matches that from the Wald papers (Ashtekar et al. [8] use the Palatini framework,
for instance).
4.2.1
Action Principle
Consider a theory of gravity which is diffeomorphism-invariant, in the sense that the laws of physics do not
change under applying a diffeomorphism to the manifold, metric and matter fields. Assume that the action
I can be expressed as an integral over the manifold M of a Lagrangian density L = Lϵ, where L is the
Lagrangian scalar:
I =
Z
M
L.
(4.2.1)
Consider a D-dimensional pseudo-Riemannian manifold M with metric gab, equipped with a Levi-Civita
connection. Assume that the action depends on the metric, the Riemann tensor and a finite number of
(symmetrized) derivatives thereof, and on matter fields and a finite number of their (symmetrized) covariant
161


## Page 205


derivatives. The matter fields are denoted collectively by ψ. (The point about symmetrization is because
antisymmetrized covariant derivatives can always be replaced with the Riemann tensor.) Denote the col-
lection of all fields, including the metric, Riemann tensor and derivatives, and matter fields and derivatives
thereof, by ϕ.
Diffeomorphism-invariant theories are ones for which the Lagrangian transforms covariantly under diffeo-
morphism f : M →M mapping the manifold into another manifold, so that the integral I is unchanged. In
technical terms, if f ∗is the pull-back associated with f, a diffeomorphism-covariant Lagrangian L satisfies
L(f ∗ϕ) = f ∗L(ϕ).
(4.2.2)
It is shown by Iyer and Wald [81] that L is diffeomorphism-covariant if the Lagrangian takes the form
L = L(gab, Rabcd, ∇a1Rabcd, . . . , ∇(a1 . . . ∇am)Rabcd, ψ, ∇a1ψ, . . . , ∇(a1 . . . ∇al)ψ),
(4.2.3)
where ψ could in principle include other (covariant) indices, contractions and so on in some way, and l and
m are finite. L, being a differential form, behaves as a covariant tensor under diffeomorphisms, as do the ϕ
fields it depends on. For an example, in pure Einstein–Hilbert gravity, L = (R −2Λ)ϵ where R is the Ricci
scalar. R depends on Rabcd and the metric, and ϵ depends on the metric only; there are no matter fields.
A variation δϕ of linear order of the fields results in a deviation of L which can be written as
δL = Eδϕ + dΘ(ϕ, δϕ).
(4.2.4)
E, which is treated as a D-form but may involve other indices to contract with possible indices in ϕ, depends
on ϕ but not on δϕ. In particular, we can break Eδϕ into terms related to g and terms related to the fields
ψ,
Eδϕ = Eab

4.2.3
Noether Current and Charge Forms
Let χ be a vector field (in general not Killing). The variation in the fields (including the metric tensor)
associated with a diffeomorphism generated by a vector χ is the Lie derivative of ϕ with respect to χ, Lχϕ.
Define the Noether current (D −1)-form Jχ associated with χ to be
Jχ = Θ(ϕ, Lχϕ) −χ · L,
(4.2.18)
where Θ(ϕ, Lχϕ) is the value of the Θ form associated with this specific variation in the fields.
The attentive reader will note that the argument δϕ in Θ(ϕ, δϕ) was assumed to be infinitesimal, whereas,
for a finite vector field χ, Lχϕ is finite. The idea is that an infinitesimal diffeomorphism is generated by χ,
so under δϕ = Lχϕδλ (for infinitesimal δλ), δL = LχLδλ, and so
LχLδλ = ELχϕδλ + Θ(ϕ, Lχϕδλ).
(4.2.19)
Then define the “finite argument” version of Θ to be
Θ(ϕ, Lχϕ) = Θ(ϕ, Lχϕδλ)

(4.2.20)

Similar arguments will apply, going forward, for any other terms where a finite argument is placed inside
some variation—that is, if the fields ϕ depend on some parameter λ in some way, as ϕ = ϕ(λ), where ϕ(0)

Cartan’s identity (0.0.36) and the fact that dL = 0 because L is fully D-dimensional and there are no

Thus if the equations of motion are satisfied, dJχ = 0, and so (at least locally) Jχ can be taken as the

4.2.4
Hamiltonian
There is a prescription for defining the Hamiltonian for the system Hχ associated with vector χ, if the
Hamiltonian exists, according to [81, 82, 130] (and related papers).
The explanation here most closely
follows [81].
169

Letting Σ be a Cauchy surface, define the pre-symplectic form Ω(ϕ, δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ) by
Ω(ϕ, δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ) =

Let the field configuration space of ϕ be F (that is, the space of possible values of ϕ).
Like ω, Ωis
antisymmetric and linear in its arguments δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ, and thus at each point ϕ acts as a 2-form, treating
δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ as infinitesimal vectors. The details are technical (see [81, 82, 130, 118]), but in short F can be
used to define the phase space of the theory, Γ, and thus the symplectic manifold, on which Ωacts as a
symplectic 2-form, which can then be used to define the Hamiltonian.
On a symplectic manifold, with generalized coordinates xA (with indices denoted by capital letters, where
xA includes both generalized space coordinates and generalized momenta), given a phase space time evolution
vector field T A and symplectic form ΩAB, the Hamiltonian H satisfies
∂H
∂xA = ΩABT B.
(4.2.41)
The equivalent of (4.2.41) for the phase space Γ for a field theory of gravity is the following. Let χa (not
necessarily Killing) be a “time-evolution vector field” for the manifold, which, despite the name, need not
necessarily be timelike (for example, it could be the azimuthal symmetry Killing vector ηa). Then associated
with this vector is a Hamiltonian Hχ, for which, if it exists, given a vacuum solution ϕ and a variation of
the fields δϕ (which does not necessarily satisfy the equations of motion),
δHχ = Ω(ϕ, δϕ, Lχϕ).
(4.2.42)
Lχϕ plays the role of the phase space time-evolution vector T A.
In its definition, Ω(ϕ, δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ) depends explicitly on the surface Σ, but if ϕ satisfies the equations of
motion and δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ satisfy the linearized equations of motion, the value of Ω(ϕ, δ1ϕ, δ2ϕ) is independent of
Cauchy surface Σ for “typical” [81] fall-off conditions on the dynamical fields.

The situation is more complicated in asymptotically anti-de Sitter spacetimes, and there are many dif-
ferent definitions of conserved energy and angular momentum, derived from a Lagrangian or Hamiltonian
method. Much of the crux of the problem comes down to the divergence of the Komar integral. A full
survey is beyond the scope of this thesis. Now I will discuss one particular paper of interest, by Barnich and
Comp`ere [13], which is useful for my purposes.
4.3
Barnich and Comp`ere
In Barnich and Comp`ere’s paper [13] (hereafter referred to as BC), they find a way to “go into the bulk”
(finite radius) for conserved quantities rather than to rely only on definitions at infinity. They use this
technique to comment on the Smarr relation for black holes in asymptotically AdS spacetime. This paper
appeared shortly after the publication of GPP and several years before the publication of CGKP. I will
present how BC approach the conserved charges in the next two sections and then present how I adapt it
beginning in Section 4.5.
179

I will introduce some notation. BC allow the Killing vector to have varying contravariant components.
Slightly adapting their notation, let the initial field configuration (including the metric) be represented by ϕ
and the varied fields be represented by ϕ+δϕ, with the metric in particular being represented by gab, gab+δgab.
The Killing vector field χa (also a symmetry of all other fields: Lχϕ = 0) is now allowed to have varying
metric components δχa. Let ¯χa be the contravariant components of χa in the unvaried spacetime, so that

4.3.1
Entering the Bulk

satisfies (4.3.29) (or, more technically, (4.3.28)). Thus this method constructs conserved charges along a path
in solution space such that the variation between two nearby points on the solution space gives the difference
in the Hamiltonian, between those two points. This can be thought of as, essentially, integrating the δHχ
from the background to the full metric, in such a way as can be done at finite radius even where the difference
between the background and full metric are very large.

BC then apply their formula to the case of black hole mechanics, and derive a Smarr relation. I will
review some salient features of black hole mechanics and get to the formulas that appear in BC.
Before continuing, I will note that a similar development occurs in papers by Hajian et al. [59, 58, 60]
who refer to the “solution phase space method,” and integrate charges via (4.3.38). The argument in [59]
relates the conserved quantities to both the Iyer–Wald formalism as well as to Barnich et al. [12, 13, 32]. The
191


## Page 235


idea is that the space of solutions is defined by different values of the parameters m and ai, and a variation
consists of varying m and ai. Then, δHχ =
H
S kEH

[δg; g] is calculated, with the expression on the right-hand
side involving terms δm and δa, and then, if the right-hand side is an exact differential in terms of δ, Hχ is
integrable. This is equivalent to
(δ1δ2 −δ2δ1)Hχ = 0,

or the strong integrability conditions of BC. [59] note that (using my notation), Hξ and Hηi are integrable,
but that Hβ is not (even calculated in BL coordinates). Additionally, they note that H2πζ/κ is integrable
(where both ζ and κ are allowed to vary).
They comment, “Similarly, it is not trivial that the charge
associated with exact symmetries ∂t or ∂φi is integrable. This point needs explicit examination,” leaving
it, for the moment, open why it is that the charges associated with ξ and ηi are integrable, but that those
associated with β are not. See also other papers by Hajian et al. in [61] and references therein, which continue
the work in interesting directions, describing the Smarr relation involving the the variation in Λ and other
coupling constants. In these papers, Λ for Kerr–AdS (and similar spacetimes) is identified as a conserved
charge, and the thermodynamic volume term is identified as conjugate to that charge. I take a different
approach, though there are similarities.
(See also [31], which considers the integrability of Hamiltonian
charges without using the Iyer–Wald formalism, for Kerr–de Sitter and Kerr–AdS.)
4.4
Black Hole Mechanics
The conventions here are mostly designed to match Comp`ere [32], with a few modifications. As in Section
3.3.2, let ζa be the Killing vector which is tangent to the null generators of a stationary black hole horizon.
(There is ambiguity in ζa, since it can always be rescaled by a constant ζa →Cζa. As in Section 3.3.2, the
choice will be made to have ζt = 1 in KS coordinates for the GKAdS solutions. ζa is thus both parallel to
and orthogonal to the horizon H, which is a null hypersurface.) Let na be a transverse null vector, which is
not tangent to (or orthogonal to) H, and which satisfies naζa = −1.

ab interpolating

4.4.3
Bifurcation Surface
The bifurcation surface of a black hole with non-degenerate horizons (κ ̸= 0) is the (D −2)-surface on the
horizon where the Killing vector tangent to the null generators vanishes. In various papers (for example,
[129, 82]), Wald and others use the bifurcation surface to make statements and proofs about black hole
thermodynamics, since many otherwise cumbersome terms are exactly zero there.
Specifically, in these
papers Wald et al. show that the first law of black hole mechanics is satisfied given the conserved charges
E, Ji defined using the covariant phase space methods, using the bifurcation surface to make statements
about horizon-related quantities such as κ and A. The disadvantage of using the bifurcation surface for
calculations for my purposes is that I will generally want to use properties of the metric in a more manifestly
stationary form, in which the Killing vectors are coordinate-independent. In this form of the metric the
Killing vectors do not vanish anywhere, which is to say that the bifurcation surface is excluded by the
coordinate charts I am considering (though not by the manifold). Additionally, the statements about the
Smarr relation made by BC which will make up the following section do not rely on using the bifurcation
surface.
(Another approach, taken by Hajian and Sheikh-Jabbari [59], is to show that the charge associated with
the Killing vector 2πζ/κ (where ζ and κ are allowed to vary) is integrable, even away from the horizon, and
in so doing they, to a degree, “do away with the horizon.” My focus is still on the horizon proper.)
4.5
Smarr Relation Revisited
In this section I provide my own commentary on the Smarr relation, first using the expressions associated
with the conserved charges for Killing vectors (ξ, ηi) corresponding to the asymptotically-static coordinates,
and then to the Killing vectors (β, ηi) corresponding to the asymptotically-rotating coordinates.

For the actual Kerr–Schild forms of the Kerr–AdS metrics (in arbitrary dimensions), the idea would then
be to start with the metric of the background in spheroidal coordinates, with the same values of ai as in
the full Kerr–AdS metric. Thus the background metric will in general depend on the rotational parameters
ai but not the mass m. hab is linear in m, and so if gab = ¯gab + hab is a vacuum solution to Einstein’s
equations with Λ with Kerr–AdS parameters (m, ai), the solution corresponding to g(s)
ab = ¯gab + shab will
be a vacuum solution to Einstein’s equations with Λ with Kerr–AdS parameters (sm, ai). While this will
be a vacuum solution to Einstein’s equations, it is not guaranteed to have all the properties we might
desire a physically reasonable solution to have. (For instance, for sufficiently small nonzero m but fixed a,
(r2 + a2)(1 + r2/l2) −2mr = 0 could have no positive real roots, corresponding to a solution with no event
horizon and thus a naked singularity, and so this will be achieved for small values of s.) It might be that
some components of the metric are not real. I will assume that we can integrate through possibly physically
unreasonable spacetimes which nevertheless are vacuum solutions to Einstein’s equations, in integrating to
define the Hamiltonian.
212


## Page 256


The advantage of this choice of path through solution space is that it allows for an expression for Cχ;γ
which is relatively simple and easily calculated, and which is defined everywhere in the manifold (not just
at infinity). This allows for conserved quantities associated with a given Killing vector to be calculated
on an arbitrary integration surface. (This was true in principle for Cχ;γ as well, but it is now easier to
calculate the result.) Because it depends on the existence of a Kerr–Schild solution, this choice for path
through solution space has limited applicability, but fortunately the Kerr–AdS (and Generalized Kerr–AdS)
spacetimes fall within it. Choosing a different path through solution space will in general lead to a different
Cχ;γ, but if BC’s strong integrability conditions hold,
H
S Cχ;γ will be independent of path. This method
gives a particularly simple form for Cχ;γ.

4.6.1
Einstein–Maxwell
It is worth considering the case of Einstein–Maxwell theory, as usual only in four dimensions. In addition
to the metric there is a Maxwell potential one-form A which must also be varied. We must be careful to
choose a parametrization of the path through solution space for both the metric and for A(s). There is
some freedom to make a gauge choice for A, and one possible choice is to identify A = 0 in pure AdS (or
Minkowski, or whatever the background of the theory is). (Since pure AdS has F = dA = 0, we really
require only that the background A be a closed form, but I will choose A = 0.) A solution consists of a
metric and potential one-form pair (gab, Aa) and we will want to interpolate between solutions (¯gab, 0) and
(gab, Aa), with intermediate solutions given by (g(s)
ab , A(s)
a ).
The Kerr–Newman–AdS electric solution for four dimensions is described in Section 3.1.1. The electro-
magnetic potential one-form A from (3.3.14) can be written in the form A = αk♭, with α = −Qr/ρ2. Let us
choose this form for A. We then have the background set (¯gab, 0) and the full metric and Maxwell one-form
is (¯gab + Hkakb, αka).

The argument in BC is that, if the strong integrability conditions hold, the conserved charges are inde-
pendent of the path found to define them, in the case where the conserved charges are vacuum solutions to
Einstein’s equations (more specifically, where the metric gab satisfies Einstein’s vacuum equations and the
variation δgab satisfies the linearized Einstein’s vacuum equations). I will now check the more general setting
where we do not demand that Einstein’s vacuum equations be satisfied. If Einstein’s equations are not

It is interesting and perhaps surprising, then, that in a Kerr–Schild spacetime with a Killing vector
and no interior singularity, the Killing energy will continue to correspond to the energy as calculated by
covariant phase space methods, since we can use Iξ. This again has to do with the fact that the Kerr–Schild
decomposition leads to the stress–energy tensor being exactly linear in the perturbation to the metric.
As alluded to above, the general argument presented in this subsection for the Killing energy is very
similar to the one appearing in Abbott and Deser [1], who construct a superpotential associated with linear-
order perturbations to a vacuum background in order to evaluate the stability of de Sitter and anti-de
Sitter spacetimes under fluctuations. There are some subtleties that distinguish their argument from the
one given above. They have a background ¯gab which is vacuum and full metric gab = ¯gab + hab and consider
contributions to the stress–energy tensor and related quantities linear in hab, which is assumed to be small
(not necessarily Kerr–Schild). They define a stress–energy tensor density T ab satisfying

E is the remaining terms in (4.8.4), which we note come down to the difference between the Komar terms
for the full and background terms and the extra term ξ · θEH.

## Page 288


Chapter 5
Notes on Black Hole Volume and Area
In this chapter, I discuss some important results related to (horizon) area and volume for the Generalized
Kerr–AdS black holes.
I begin in Section 5.1 by reviewing formulas for the area and volume, respectively, of higher dimensional
unit spheres and balls. I perform the (well-known) calculation for the “area” (N-volume) of an N-sphere
explicitly because it involves some integrals which will recur throughout Chapter 6.
I then turn to the Generalized Kerr–AdS spacetimes. In Section 5.2, I use the pseudo-Cartesian coor-
dinates from Section 3.4 to calculate in an intuitive way the vector volume associated with ξ = ∂t inside a
region r ≤R for these spacetimes. I then calculate the area of the black horizon explicitly in Section 5.3, the
content of which is moved to Appendix A. In section 5.4, I review the Euclidean action method calculation
as used by GPP and show how the vector volume arises naturally.
I then resolve two of the outstanding questions mentioned in the previous chapter. In Section 5.5, I
give an explanation for why the Killing potential for ζ constructed from the PCKY tensor as introduced by
CGKP satisfies (4.1.56). Finally, in Section 5.6, I use the PCKY tensor to explain why the vector volume
satisfies Vξ = r+A/(D −1) for the GKAdS spacetimes.

5.2
Volume Calculations Simplified
The volume calculations for the Generalized Kerr–AdS spacetimes can be simplified by showing that, when we
use the pseudo-Cartesian coordinates, the vector volume with respect to ξ is equal to the Euclidean volume
of the region. This argument was given in the four-dimensional case in Section 2.7.3, but the following
generalizes to higher dimensions using the same principle.
Consider the GKAdS spacetime in KS coordinates (see Chapter 3). Let ξ = ∂/∂t and let R be the region
consisting of −∞< t < ∞, xi ∈Σ where xi represent all the xa coordinates except for t. The vector volume
(see Chapter 2) is then

Z

√−¯gdD−1x.
(5.2.2)
It is now useful to find the most convenient coordinate system in which to evaluate the vector volume for
the background spacetime. Rather than the spheroidal coordinates, it is useful to use the pseudo-Cartesian
coordinates of Section 3.4, in the coordinate system (t, xi, yi, z) = (t, Xα) with metric (3.4.13). This gives

Thus all we need to do to calculate the volume of some region is to calculate the Euclidean volume once

even more obvious way if the background is Minkowski rather than AdS (so that it will also apply to volume
calculations for Kerr or Myers–Perry black holes).
The black hole horizon is at a surface of constant r. It is then useful to consider surfaces of constant r. The
surface of constant r is a (D −1)-dimensional ellipsoid in the coordinates (xi, yi, z), satisfying (3.4.7). From
(3.4.7), the semi-major axes are two copies each of

volume of a (D −1)-dimensional ellipsoid is the volume of the unit (D −1)-ball times the product of the
(D −1) semi-major axes (see e.g. [86]). The reason is essentially that an ellipsoid is a linear rescaling of a
sphere, where each dimension is rescaled by one of the semi-major axes. Thus the interior Euclidean volume
of the ellipsoid to the interior of the r-constant surface is

5.3
Area Calculation for Generalized Kerr–AdS
Here I find the black hole horizon area for Generalized Kerr–AdS metrics. The values for the horizon area
for Kerr–AdS were found by Gibbons et al. [51, 52]. I thought it worthwhile to perform my own calculation,
which additionally covers the Generalized Kerr–AdS case. Along the way I encounter an integral of a form
which will recur in Chapter 6. This calculation appeared in the main body of my thesis, but it is sufficiently
involved that I have relegated it to Appendix A here.
The result is that for the generalized Kerr–AdS
spacetimes, (3.3.52) is recovered.
254


## Page 298


5.4
Euclidean Action
GPP [53] used the Euclidean Action method for calculating the black hole mass, in higher dimensions. The
Euclidean Action method was initially applied to (four-dimensional) Schwarzschild–AdS thermodynamics by
Hawking and Page in [66]. As I will show, the vector volume arises in a natural way from GPP’s calculation.
I will review GPP’s calculation and then provide my own commentary in Section 5.4.1. They use Kerr–AdS
in ABL coordinates, with metric given by (3.1.24). Then the metric is Euclideanized, in the sense of changing
the signature of the metric to a Euclidean (as opposed to Lorentzian) signature. Euclidean time τE and
rotational parameters αi are defined by τ = −iτE, ai = iαi, so that the metric is recast in Euclidean form.
From now on I’ll drop the E subscript. The horizon is at V (r)−2m = 0, or r = r+. The metric is smooth at
the horizon, in the sense that conical singularities are avoided, if τ is identified with periodicity β = 2π/κ,
where κ is the surface gravity. β is the inverse temperature of the black hole and is not to be confused with
the β Killing vector.

Chapter 6
Explicit Calculation of Charge Integrals for
Generalized Kerr–AdS
In this chapter, I apply the quantity Iχ defined in Chapter 4 to the Generalized Kerr–AdS metrics to describe
the energy (or enthalpy) and angular momentum. In doing so, I address the questions: “why it is necessary
to use a conserved charge based on ξ rather than β in order to recover a horizon variation law?” and “why
does the volume occur naturally in the Smarr relation for the conserved quantity associated with β, rather
than with ξ”?
I give an overview of the values for the energy and angular momentum in Section 6.1. Then in Section
6.2 I explicitly calculate the value of Iχ for various values of χ in the Generalized Kerr–AdS case, including
Kerr–Newman–AdS. I show that for solutions of Einstein’s equations (Kerr–AdS and Kerr–Newman–AdS)
the

H

method or the AMD conformal methods.

The overall thrust of these sections, specialized to four dimensions, is to show why
H
H Iχ makes sense
as a conserved quantity constructed to produce a metric variation law, and why it applies particularly for
vacuum and with ξ, η rather than β, η—or, in other words, why it is that it is necessary to use charges
constructed from ξ, η rather than β, η. (In the context of the papers listed in Section 1.4, this amounts to
another demonstration that charges associated with ξ and η are integrable, but that the one associated with
β is not; in the language of BC, this is due to the failure of the strong integrability conditions associated
with Qβ;γ.)
In Section 6.5, I calculate the explicit values for
H
Iχ for χ = β, ξ, ηi and ζ on surfaces of constant t, r in
arbitrary dimension D ≥4. I show how the calculation of Iβ simplifies considerably due to the properties of
β introduced in Chapter 3. Breaking Iβ down into terms related to the Komar terms (the Komar integral
minus the Komar integral for the background) and the non-Komar integral, I simplify expressions down to
show as cleanly as possibly why, for β in particular as opposed to other Killing vectors, the Komar term is
D −3 times the non-Komar term, in vacuum. This addresses, according to the arguments in Chapter 4, why
the vector volume appears naturally in the Smarr relation constructed from β, but not the one constructed
with ξ.
In Section 6.6, I discuss, in four dimensions, a physical process version of the first law and how this can
266


## Page 310


also be used to describe the conserved quantities in Kerr–AdS, in a way that dovetails with other results in
this chapter.
A note I want to make at the start of this chapter is that in Kerr (in four dimensions) or Myers–Perry
spacetimes (in higher dimensions), which are the Kerr–AdS limit with Λ = 0 or l →∞, the Komar integral
associated with the background spacetime is zero, because Komar integrals are zero in Minkowski space.
Further, since β = ξ + l−2aη, in Kerr, β = ξ, and so the ξ and β conserved quantities coincide.
The
combination of the two simplifies matters greatly. The vanishing of the background Komar integral is the
reason that there is no volume term appearing in the Smarr relation for Kerr/Myers–Perry, and the fact
that ξ = β explains why the distinction between ξ and β, so important in the Kerr–AdS case, does not
materialize in Kerr/Myers–Perry.

Of particular interest is the even-dimensional case, where the “last” Killing vector
∂
∂ψn−1 has 2(n −1) =
D −2, so that HI
∂ψn−1 = 0. It is unclear whether there is any physical significance to the disappearance
of the charge associated with ∂/∂ψn−1 in even dimensions. None of the charges associated with the ∂/∂ψj
vanish in odd dimensions (in general).
In D = 4, in (r, θ, ψj) coordinates there are only two linearly independent Killing vectors associated with
the time–azimuthal sector, β = ∂/∂ψ0 and ∂/∂ψ1. Of this set, β gives a nonzero charge and ∂/∂ψ1 gives
a zero charge. The associated charges for ξ and η can be found entirely in terms of the decomposition of ξ
and η in ψj coordinates: E = Fξψ0 and J = −Fηψ0.
Some more commentary (which did not appear in the thesis) on HI
ψj is included in Section 6.5.7.

6.2
Explicit Calculation of Charge Integrals: Four Dimensions
To give a little bit of extra information, I will find the solutions for a “generalized Kerr–anti-de Sitter” case,
which replaces the constant m with a general function of r, m →µ(r). The main other physically interesting
solution that is included in this form is the four-dimensional Kerr–Newman solution, but it is my hope that
solving for an arbitrary function µ(r) might give some hints as to which aspects of the Kerr–anti-de Sitter
solution arise because of the Kerr–Schild form and which arise specifically from the vacuum case. I will
report results for the four-dimensional case in this section.
Because the topology is so unusual in the Kerr–NUT–AdS case (let alone a generalized Kerr–NUT–AdS
case) I will not be including any NUT parameters.
In four dimensions, the calculations can be completed using GRTensorIII [56]. In arbitrary dimensions
more care is required. The arbitrary-dimensional case will be considered in Section 6.5.

6.3
Variations in Four Dimensions
In this subsection I consider the variations in the various quantities calculated in Section 6.2, and under
what circumstances we recover the horizon variation law δE = ΩδJ + κδA/8π.
This breaks down into a number of checks, coming down to checking:
• Under what circumstances is δIχ = kEH
¯χ
[δg; g]?
• Under what circumstances is
H
H kEH
¯χ
[δ; g] = κδA/8π?
(In this section, Iχ is always assumed to mean IEH
χ
.) The combination of the two questions of course checks
whether or not we can use
H
H δIχ = κδA/8π. The general conclusions of this section are: that we can
generally use δIχ = kEH
¯χ
[δg; g] provided that δχa = 0 and δ¯gab = 0, and under specific circumstances when
δ¯gab ̸= 0 as well; and that we can use
H
H kEH
¯χ
[δ; g] = κδA/8π under a wide variety of circumstances, so that
we can more or less trust this formula to hold under the situations we are considering.
Along the way, I will put forward the argument that the reason why the variation law fails when it is
constructed from β rather than ξ has to do with the fact that, in most frames, the components of βa vary
when comparing two different values of a (or l); and, further, that in an asymptotically rotating frame, ¯gab
changes too much under a variation of δa to be able to use δIχ = kEH
¯χ
[δg; g]. This gives an answer to the
question of why it is preferable to use an asymptotically static vector ξ.
The variations are performed in four dimensions for computational and conceptual simplicity. I believe
that the conclusions generalize to higher dimensions in a straightforward way.
277


## Page 321


(Since the writing of my thesis, I found that Jing and Peng [85] examine charges in the off-shell generalized
Abbott–Deser–Tekin formalism, which has a similar form to the charges that I integrate with my form, and
also examine the consequences of allowing either m only, or m and a, to vary, in both a frame which is
asymptotically static and a frame which is non-rotating, and they find that charges are indeed integrable
when associated with, in my notation, ξ but not with β, when applied to variations in both m and a. On
the other hand, the charges for β are also integrable when only m is varied, but are not integrable of both
m and a are varied. These match up with my results here.)
6.3.1
Variations Involving the Mass Function Only
To begin with, consider a variation in which the mass function µ(r) changes, but a and l remain constant.
Let us call this variation ˆδ, so that ˆδa = ˆδl = 0, ˆδµ(r) ̸= 0 in general. The meaning of this is that µ(r) and
(µ + ˆδµ)(r) are both functions of r, so that at every point there is some nonzero ˆδµ(r), but ˆδµ(r1) ̸= ˆδµ(r2)
for r1 ̸= r2 in general. We also demand that the variation leave the coordinates themselves unaffected.
For variations involving µ(r) only, the background metric ¯gab has a zero variation, since ¯gab does not
depend on µ(r). Similarly, the null vector ka (in both covariant and contravariant components) has ˆδka =
ˆδka = 0. Finally, we have ˆδξa = ˆδηa = ˆδβa = 0, since none of these vectors depend on µ(r).
As discussed in Section 4.6.3, δIχ = kEH

There are two effects that we see when δa changes, both of which contribute to the failure of the variation
law.
The first is that the background metric varies if δa ̸= 0.
The second is that the components of
βa = δa
t + al−2δa
ϕ also vary under the variation in a, so that δβa ̸= 0, though δξa = 0. I aim to show that it
is the result of the background metric variation that the first law fails for both ˜E(r) and ˜F(r) in general, but
E = ˜E(r) when µ′(r) = 0. It is because δβa ̸= 0 that the variation law fails for F = ˜F(r) even if µ′(r) = 0.
The argument for this is subtle and will be teased out in the rest of this section. I will make a few
preliminary comments. The first is that while the background metric is changing, the background metric
is still the spacetime associated with AdS with constant l—it is just that the coordinate system changes,
since it depends explicitly on a. One way to look at it is that while the t and ϕ coordinates retain their
meanings, the interpretations of the coordinates r and θ depend on a, when comparing to an a-independent
background such as the spherical polar coordinate system or pseudo-Cartesian coordinates. The meaning of
a “constant-r surface” thus, in some senses, changes, as the constant-r surface moves in the spherical polar or
pseudo-Cartesian background. One way to account for this is to perform calculations entirely in a coordinate
frame where the background metric does not change. This will be the subject of the next subsection: what
happens if a varies, but we find a way to keep ¯gab unchanging? The advantage of this is that it will allow
us to disentangle the effect of the changing components of βa from the effect of the changing components of
¯gab.
285


## Page 329


6.3.3
Constant Background Metric with Variable Rotation Parameter
Here I consider the case where the background metric does not change, even as a changes. There are two
basic approaches, which I will consider in turn. The first is to use a set of coordinates for the AdS background
which do not depend on a. The second is to use a set of coordinates for the AdS background which depend
only on the unperturbed value of a. The latter is a little more conceptually involved but turns out to be
easier to work with computationally. I will begin with the former case.
Two coordinate systems for AdS I have already introduced which do not depend on a are the spherical
polar coordinates (3.1.13) and the pseudo-Cartesian coordinates from Section 3.4. It is possible to write r
and θ—or, more conveniently, u = cos θ—in terms of the spherical polar or pseudo-Cartesian coordinates
in closed form, but that closed form involves nested radicals, which become computationally intensive. The
workaround here is either to work at large values of r where the expressions for r and u simplify considerably,
or, as I will do later on, to use a form of the spheroidal coordinates with the unvaried a parameter a(0).
To explain my approach more clearly, I will start with some physically uninteresting examples motivated
by their computational simplicity.
Consider a metric with (four-dimensional) Minkowski background in
Cartesian coordinates,

We can thus interpret δIχ = kEH
χ
[δg; g] for any variations which keep δ¯gab = 0 and have confirmed it
in a few specific cases. The problem with this line of argument is that it is less convenient to keep the
background metric ¯gab fixed than it is to use the spheroidal coordinates which in general depend on the
rotational parameter a. This does however point at a significant advantage of using the background metric
which is asymptotically static, in the form t, ϕ, over the form of the background in which the metric is
asymptotically non-static. While the definitions of the spheroidal r and u depend implicitly on a in either
case, the definitions of t and ϕ are fixed with respect to the fundamental symmetries of the spacetime.
I will note before continuing that this “workaround” to show δIχ = kEH
χ
[δg; g] was doable if a is varied
specifically because the spacetime is still AdS, and so it is possible to find coordinates where the background
does not depend on a.
It is simply not true if l is allowed to vary, because l appears not only in the
background metric but also in the curvature invariants associated with that background (most obviously in
R = 4Λ = −12/l2) and so no choice of coordinates, no matter how judicious, can remove the change in the
background metric. I want to emphasize that this section is meant as a check—I already showed in Section
4.6.3 that δIχ = kEH
χ
[δg; g] if δχa = δ¯gab = 0 for Kerr–Schild form. What is useful about this exercise is
to show how the result δIχ ̸= kEH
χ
[δg; g] if a varies and we compare different Kerr–AdS solutions can be
avoided if we do use a constant background. The cost is that it is more computationally intensive to write
the spheroidal r and u = cos θ in terms of the “fixed” spherical polar R, U coordinates.
The other option, which I will discuss in Section 6.4, is to use the background metric associated with
unvaried parameter a(0) and find a way to transform the final background metric back to the metric associated
with a(0).
We now turn to verify the horizon area rule.

6.4
Representing Infinitesimal Coordinate Transformations with the Lie Deriva-
tive
A gap in our argument so far is that
H
H kEH
¯ζ
[δg; g] = κδA/8π was proven by [32], following [11], to hold
provided the horizon has the same coordinates in the initial and final spacetime, but appears to hold for
H

+ are different. Further, the
statement δI ¯ζ = kEH
¯ζ
[δg; g] relies on the background metric being unchanging, but we still seem to be able
to use the integral equation
H
H δI ¯ζ =
H
H kEH
¯ζ
[δg; g] even if δ¯gab ̸= 0, albeit only in vacuum rather than in
full generality. The former issue can be addressed by moving to coordinates where the radial coordinate is
rescaled, so that the horizon remains at the same location, as in Section 6.3.4, and the latter issue can be
addressed by moving to coordinates where the background metric remains constant, as in Section 6.3.3, but
generally we cannot satisfy both requirements at once.
In this section I give an explanation for what currently seems to be very fortuitous, that both equalities
H
δI ¯ζ =
H
H kEH
¯ζ
[δg; g] = κδA/8π appear to hold in a more general setting than one might expect.
To do so I use the Lie derivative and its connection to infinitesimal diffeomorphisms/coordinate transfor-
mations. (As pointed out by, e.g., Wald [127], we can either take the active view of diffeomorphisms, in which
a diffeomorphism f mapping points in M to other points in M also defines a map acting on tensors, or the
passive view, in which the points and tensors are unchanged, but f is associated with a coordinate transfor-
mation, affecting the coordinate representations of the points and tensor components. These points of view
are philosophically different but functionally work out pretty similarly, so I won’t dwell on the distinction.)
To begin, when considering a metric variation δgab, there are two parts to consider: the part of the change
in gab which results from moving between two distinct spacetimes, and the part of δgab which is “pure gauge,”
and only the result of a diffeomorphism/coordinate transformation. The latter can be connected to the Lie
derivative (see, for instance, [127, 115, 137]) and will be the focus of this section.
I will, roughly following the above references, lay out the argument in the following way, taking the
passive (coordinate transformation) point of view. Let M be a spacetime manifold and let xa be a set of
coordinates for it, with metric tensor field whose components depend on the coordinates: gab = gab(xc).
Let ˜xa = ϕa(xb) be a new set of coordinates, with functions ϕa each functions of xa. Schematically we can
represent this as ˜x = ϕ(x), and so ϕ represents a map. We demand that the coordinate transformation be

6.4.2
Using Infinitesimal Coordinate Transformations to Keep the Kerr–Schild Background Fixed
In Section 6.3.3, I considered the case where the background metric was independent of rotation parameter
a, in order to assure δ¯gab = 0. One way to address the GKAdS case where a is allowed to vary is to perform a
coordinate transformation so that the background metric always depends on the initial rotational parameter
a(0).
Consider the KS form of the metric, and let g(s)
ab be a family of GKAdS spacetimes, parametrized by s,
given by
g(s)
ab = ¯g(s)
ab + H(s)k(s)
a k(s)
b .
(6.4.68)
The values for the background metric and k(s)
a
are given by (3.1.16), with a = a(s), in general dependent on
s. H(s) = 2µ(s)(r)/U. For convenience in calculation I will work in coordinates (t, r, u, ϕ), where u = cos θ.
In this section, l is kept fixed.

since g(s)

(6.4.70)
I will now give an argument for constructing a coordinate system in which the background metric does not

What is the takeaway from this section? ˆζ = β +ω(0)η is the Killing vector tangent to the null generators
in the unperturbed spacetime whose components are constant in a frame in which β, η have constant com-
ponents. It turns out that
H
H kEH
ˆζ
[δg; g] = κδA/8π still holds if we use coordinates xa such that ˆζa has the
same contravariant components throughout the variation, which is to say a set of asymptotically rotating
(BL or BLKS) coordinates. Nothing breaks down about the
H
H kEH
ˆζ
[δg; g] = κδA/8π formula. However, the
other part of the equivalences, namely the requirement that the variation in I ˆζ produce kEH
ˆζ
[δg; g], fails, even
in vacuum. This is because even though we can perform an infinitesimal coordinate transformation to (˜x(s))a
coordinates so that ¯g(s)
ab is unchanging, doing so automatically causes (˜ˆζ(s))a to have varying contravariant
components.
This all comes down to the following: when comparing spacetimes with different a(s) parameters, we
can, through a judicious choice of coordinates, either find coordinates where ˆζ has constant contravariant
components or where the background metric ¯gab has constant components, but not both.
Because the
statement δIχ = kEH
χ
[δg; g] requires that δ¯gab = 0 and that δχa = 0, we cannot use ˆζ as the vector on which
to base a variation—or, in other words, we cannot use β as one of our “fixed” vectors, if we are varying a.
This gives a slightly different perspective on the failure of the variation law.
In this perspective, it
is not so much that using an asymptotically rotating frame is itself a problem, because we can still use
H
H kEH
ˆζ
[δg; g] = κδA/8π in an asymptotically rotating frame. However, the conserved charges resulting
from integrating through solution space do not work when they are based on components which are fixed
324


## Page 368


in the asymptotically rotating frame, because the vector β = ξ + l−2a(s)η points in different directions,
with respect to the background metric ¯gab, as a function of a(s). If we choose coordinates where β has
constant components, the background metric changes its components, and if we choose coordinates where
the background metric components do not change as we compare solutions, then β changes its components.
By contrast, ξ (and vectors based around ξ, such as ¯ζ = ξ + Ω(0)η) can have fixed contravariant components
in coordinates in which the metric does not depend on s. This means that we can find coordinates such
that δIξ = kEH
ξ
[δg; g], but not δIβ = kEH
β
[δg; g]. This addresses the question of why it is necessary to use
ξ rather than β in the construction of conserved charges.
Next I turn to a breakdown of the conserved charge in arbitrary dimension.

6.5.2
Terms Associated with Principal Vector
For the iK terms, it will be useful to start by looking at (iK
β )ab, where several terms will cancel or simplify
very nicely due to some of the particular properties of the β vector. I will remind the reader of some of the
properties that β, the Killing vector associated with the Principal Conformal Killing–Yano tensor, has:
• It is a Killing vector, with components only in the “time-and-azimuthal” sector of the metric.
• It has a time component of 1 (βt = 1).
• It has βaka = 1 (3.3.3).
• It has ∇kβ ∝k (3.3.5) and ¯∇kβ ∝k (3.3.43).
Of course the first two properties are shared by many of the Killing vectors under consideration, but the
latter two are particular to β, and, as shown in Section 3.3.1, follow from the fact that ka is one of the null
eigenvectors of h, affinely parametrized. These properties will turn out to simplify the expression for the
Komar term associated with β considerably.
Two other properties of β which are related to the above which I think are also suggestive of its key role,
but which I do not use directly below, are
332


## Page 376


• It is proportional to the leading term in the “time–angular” part of k, from (3.3.6), so that at large r
(using the large-r form of X), k ≃∂r −l2
r2 β; and
• Using (3.2.80) ∇aβb and thus the Komar integrand can be related directly to the PCKY tensor and
to the Riemann tensor and its decomposition. In particular, for an AdS background with a vacuum
Kerr–AdS full spacetime, Ra
b = ¯Ra
b = −(D −1)l−2δa
b so that

tensor, for Kerr–AdS specifically. This is not true for just any Killing vector χ but is specifically due
to the symmetries of β following from h. I elaborate on this in Chapter 8 (which did not appear in
the thesis).
It is not so much that one couldn’t use these last two points to draw conclusions about what makes β special
as that I emphasized the first few points instead. There are so many reasons that β is special that it is hard
to tease out which are the most important.

The most sensible coordinates to use are either yα coordinates for the latitude angles or µi (with one

geometric interpretations are clearer. We have Un expressed in terms of yα but also have U expressed in

Thus we recover (4.5.13)! That the Komar and non-Komar terms are equal, up to a numerical factor, when
the charge related to β is used, is the basic reason why the volume appears in a natural way in the Smarr
relationship for β and thus F, as discussed in Section 4.5.2. This equality follows from the simplifications
that result from the properties of β, listed at the beginning of this section. This is one of the main results
of this thesis.
It might seem surprising that the asymptotically-rotating Killing vector is the one for which the Komar
and non-Komar terms have such a simple relationship. Note however that Kerr–AdS permits a smooth limit
to Kerr (or Myers–Perry, in higher dimensions) by taking l →∞. In this case, we still have (6.5.75), but
here β = ξ = ∂
∂t, since all the terms ai/l2 →0. The idea then is that the Principal Vector is the same as the
asymptotically-static vector in Kerr/Myers–Perry.
It is plain to see that, for generic µ(r) (even with the asymptotic form µ(r) = m∞+ P
j d∞
j=1r−j), the
equality (6.5.73) does not hold on an arbitrary surface C, in general. One need only compare (6.5.64) or
(6.5.65) to (6.5.16). One way to put it is that the relationship at infinity is guaranteed (provided regularity
of µ(r)), and that it is only in vacuum when the Komar and non-Komar terms maintain their values “from
infinity” to a generic integration surface.
If µ(r) is not constant and we evaluate at finite radius, the contributions from the Komar and non-Komar
terms associated with µ′(r) do not combine or cancel in any simple way. The total charge in this case is,

6.5.6
“Co-rotating” Killing Vector Charge
Here I will state some results for the full conserved charge associated with the vector ζ, and a more general
form of it. As I will show, the term µ′(r) (the derivative of the scalar mass function) drops out of these
expressions entirely, due to a fortuitous cancellation of terms. An obvious generalization of ζ is a vector
proportional to eˆn, that is to say, in the direction of the orthonormal frame vector which points in the
direction of ζ on the horizon.
In arbitrary dimension, we have (Iχ)c1...cD−2 = iab
χ ϵabc1...cD−2. Using hab = Hkakb, we can rewrite iχ

6.6
Physical Process Version
As pointed out in, e.g., [50, 115], there is also a “physical process” version of the first law of BH mechanics.
In this case, instead of comparing distinct spacetimes, the same spacetime is allowed to vary with time, in
that a small amount of matter is allowed to pass by the BH horizon. This is done as a quasi-stationary
process, so that the black hole remains nearly stationary throughout. Following the explanation in [115], the
argument is (briefly) as follows.
Let a four-dimensional black hole, initially stationary (not necessarily vacuum), be perturbed by a small
amount of matter flowing through the horizon. Assume that after the small amount of matter flow, the
horizon settles down to a new stationary state. The initial and final black holes will both have a stationarity
Killing vector ξ and azimuthal symmetry Killing vector η. Let the event horizon be given by H. Let v be
a parameter along the null generators of the horizon, with v = −∞in the distant past and v = +∞in the
distant future. (In general v will not be affine.) Assume that the black hole is quasi-stationary for both
v = ±∞.
Let, following Poisson (modifying notation), let the change in black hole mass δEH and change in black
hole angular momentum δJH be given by, respectively,
δEH = −
Z

Poisson works in first order in T a

The argument that the transfer of energy can be written in the above form is roughly the following. Let
εa = −T a
b ξb and ℓa = T a
b ηb be vector fields where T ab is a test stress–energy tensor that does not influence
the spacetime. We can interpret εa as an energy-density flux vector and ℓa as an angular momentum-flux
vector, based on the decomposition of the stress–energy tensor T ab. If ξa and ηa are Killing vectors, then
371

Since ∇aT ab = 0, T ab is symmetric, and ∇(aξb) = 0 is the Killing condition, this vanishes. The argument
for ∇aℓa = 0 is analogous. Then from Gauss’ law, the amount of flux of εa flowing into any region must be
equal to the amount flowing out, so it can be interpreted as the flux of energy (similarly for ℓand angular
momentum). It is not necessarily the case that this interpretation corresponds to the same interpretation
as energy and angular momentum for the black hole, but we will accept this idea for the moment. I include
the subscripts H to try to emphasize that these are the energy and angular momentum transfers specifically
across the horizon according to (6.6.1).

Another note I want to make, before continuing, is that because ¯ζa¯ζa = 0 (and remains so throughout
the spacetime, because we presume that the deviation of ¯ζa from being null is only first order in the change
in the stress–energy tensor), if Gab +Λgab = 8πTab, this implies that Gab¯ζa¯ζb = 8πTab¯ζa¯ζb, as Λgab¯ζa¯ζb = 0.
I wanted to check how the δEH, δJH related to the E, J quantities associated with Kerr–AdS. My thinking
is that since we have δEH −ΩδJH = κδA/8π, if we could show that EH = E, JH = J for some values E, J ,
that we would have values of E, J that would automatically satisfy the first law. This is another approach
373


## Page 417


that amounts to integrating the first law, in a manner similar to GPP—and also in a manner similar to
integrating kEH
χ
to get δE, δJi.
The idea of interpreting the energy and angular momentum in a spacetime according to the amount
that must be transferred across some boundary surface when comparing two different spacetimes has a long
history. To give a key example, much of the argument behind the AMD formalism [6, 7, 8] relies on showing
how the change in AMD integral values corresponds to the stress–energy tensor flux through a hypersurface
at infinity. I have not seen someone make the precise argument that I am laying out here, but both the
results and my ability to position it within the broader literature (as well as its overall uniqueness) are best
considered tentative, hence its inclusion as a final section in this chapter.
Here is a specific method I considered. First I will consider the Kerr spacetime, which is simpler and
more familiar, and then Kerr–AdS spacetime, and then some other spacetimes of interest which I did not
consider elsewhere in the thesis.
The calculations in the following subsections are performed/checked using GRTensorIII.
6.6.1
Kerr
The Kerr metric in ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates is given by [115] (for ease of computation
using u = cos θ)

As a toy model, consider the case where m and a are allowed to vary as functions of v. That means
that every constant-v surface corresponds to a constant-v surface in a Kerr spacetime, but for different
Kerr spacetimes. I then let m(−∞) = m0, a(−∞) = a0 and m(+∞) = m1, a(+∞) = a1, both constant.

the Vaidya spacetime generalizes Schwarzschild by writing Schwarzschild in ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein

My initial thought was that it made the most sense to have a treatment in the ingoing Eddington–
Finkelstein coordinates both because the horizon is regular in these coordinates and that the layout with v

6.6.2
Other Vacuum Spacetimes with Zero Cosmological Constant
I was interested by how effective this strategy of checking the stress–energy tensor was at other spacetimes
which are in Λ = 0 vacuum. Here I show two examples: the Curzon–Chazy metric in Stachel coordinates,
and the five dimensional black ring spacetime. I will not attempt to describe the black hole horizon for these
spacetimes and am only evaluating the energy and angular momentum transfer terms associated with the
stress–energy tensor, and how they relate to the ADM and Komar quantities.
Curzon–Chazy
The Curzon–Chazy metric is a Λ = 0 vacuum form of the Weyl metric.
It can be written in Stachel
