6.6.3
Kerr–AdS
I work in four dimensions only for simplicity.
Here I follow the basic pattern laid out in Section 6.6.1 for the Kerr case, beginning with the case where
Λ is held constant, where we find many analogous results.
In particular I will show that when we use
ABL coordinates, we find exact time derivatives associated with dEΣ/dτ, dJΣ/dτ which correspond to the
expected E, J values, and when we use a similar “ingoing Kerr–AdS–Vaidya” type spacetime we get similar
expressions with extra terms. I also show that if we use the BL form of the metric we do not get the same
clean results, further bolstering the argument that the ABL asymptotically static frame is better suited to
describing the conserved quantities. The main difference, which we expect, is that unlike with Kerr, Curzon–
Chazy or the Black Ring spacetime, the Komar integral associated with ξ does not reproduce the conserved
energy.
Having gone through the case where Λ does not vary, I consider the case where it is allowed to vary, via

effectively being time-dependent. Without a specific physical theory which would give rise to the changing
value of Λ, it is difficult to say what the relationship between the Einstein tensor and stress–energy tensor

395


## Page 439


6.6.7
Physical Process Version Summary
Results here are tentative but this suggests that we can use the approach of looking at the flux of the stress–
energy tensor for slow variations which transform one solution in a class of solutions to another (e.g. one
Kerr–AdS solution to another) in order to integrate out conserved quantities. I find it interesting that we
can recover Θ by writing dE/dv = dE/dv −ΘdΛ/dv for the Kerr–AdS case with varying Λ, and also that
the close relationship between the flux of angular momentum and angular momentum Komar integral gives
further justification for the use of the Komar integral for angular momentum. Finally by showing how, in
metrics of the form (6.6.76), the √−gGr
bχb/8π terms match up with the kEH
χ
[dg(s)/ds; g] terms, I connected
this section to the rest of the thesis.
An obvious next step would be to consider the Kerr–Newman and Kerr–Newman–AdS solution, in this
case hopefully separating out the “matter” and electromagnetic stress–energy tensor components in some
way. I have done some early work on the Kerr–NUT spacetime as well. I would like to understand better
the relationship between the above quantities and a more physically motivated model of a dynamic horizon,
particularly one with varying Λ.
396


## Page 440


Chapter 7
Conclusion
The central concern of this thesis is the role of the volume in black hole thermodynamics. In Chapter 2
I defined the vector volume for a region R and a divergence-free vector va such that va is tangent to the
boundary ∂R as the rate of growth of the D-dimensional volume of R along the flow of v. I showed that
this is a constant, and that it applies particularly if v is a Killing vector. I showed that in coordinates
adapted to the vector v such that va = δa
0, the vector volume can be written as
R √−gdD−1x, where g is
the determinant of the metric and dD−1x is the product of the coordinate differentials with dx0 omitted.
For a Kerr–Schild spacetime, the background and full metric determinants are the same, showing that the
vector volume for the full and background spacetimes are equal. For spacetimes where the background is
Minkowski space, such as the Kerr spacetime, and where the vector in question is the staticity Killing vector
in the Minkowski background, this implies the interpretation of the vector volume as the Euclidean volume
as calculated in the Cartesian coordinates in the Minkowski background. I showed that the vector volume is
linear with respect to the vector and that the vector volume associated with an azimuthal symmetry vector
vanishes for an azimuthally symmetric region.
I showed that for the Kerr–Newman–AdS or Kerr–Newman–de Sitter black holes, a canonical black hole
volume can be defined by using the black hole region between the singularity and the (outer) event horizon
and using the canonically normalized stationarity Killing vector. I showed that this canonical black hole
volume, denoted by VC or Vξ,B, is equal to volumes which had previously appeared in the literature, especially
the Parikh volume [111] and the geometric volume appearing in [34].
397


## Page 441


In Chapter 3, I stated some important facts about the higher-dimensional Kerr–AdS solutions and their
place in the larger set of Generalized Kerr–NUT–AdS spacetimes. In addition to stating some important
properties of the PCKY tensor h and associated principal vector β needed in future chapters, I showed how
the Jacobi transformations by [26] applied to the µi from the original higher-dimensional solutions from [52]
to the yα parameters worked because they were in effect “finishing the job” of the Jacobi transformation as
applied to the νi, counting xn = ir as one of the xµ parameters, and showed how the Generalized Kerr–AdS
solutions can be written in pseudo-Cartesian coordinates.
In Chapter 4, I reviewed how the papers by Gibbons, Perry and Pope [53] and Cvetiˇc, Gibbons, Kubizˇn´ak
and Pope [34] laid out an argument for a first law variation. The GPP paper proposed that a definition of
energy E for Kerr–AdS black holes must satisfy the first law δE = P
i ΩiδJi+TδS where TδS = κδA/8π, with
black hole angular momenta Ji, angular velocities Ωi, temperature T, entropy S, surface gravity κ and area A.
They obtained values of E by integrating the first law and found that it was necessary to use an asymptotically
static frame in order to get a working first law. The CGKP paper then allowed ˜Λ to vary, interpreting E
as an enthalpy, and showed that this resulted in the presence of a thermodynamic volume Vth, with (in the
presence of charges Qα and potentials Φα) δE = P
i ΩiδJi + P
α ΦαδQα + TδS + VthδP, where the pressure
P is proportional to ˜Λ. They point out that this follows directly from a scaling relation and the existence of
a Smarr relation, written, for dimension D, (D−3)E = (D−2) (P
i ΩiJi + TS)+(D−3) P
α ΦαQα −2VthP.
They also showed that if, instead (using my notation) F, the “energy/enthalpy” associated with the principal
vector β rather than the asymptotically static vector ξ, with corresponding angular velocity ωi, is used, the
Smarr relation is (D −3)F = (D −2) (P
i ΩiJi + TS) + (D −3) P
α ΦαQα −2VgeoP, where Vgeo is the
geometric volume, which is the one which corresponds to Vξ,B.
They also pointed out the relation for
Kerr–AdS Vgeo = r+A/(D −1), where r+ is the value of the spheroidal radius on the horizon.
To address why it was necessary to use the conserved charge associated with the asymptotically static
Killing vector ξ rather than the principal vector β to construct a first law relation and why Vξ,B appears
more naturally in the Smarr relation associated with β rather than ξ, I gave a new definition for a conserved
charge, making use of the covariant phase space methods of Wald and the idea from Barnich and Comp`ere to
integrate through solution space, in concert with the Kerr–Schild decomposition of the Kerr–AdS spacetimes.
It is possible that the Relativity community was not clamouring for another definition of a conserved charge
associated with a Killing vector, one only applicable to Kerr–Schild spacetimes, but it possesses many
398


## Page 442


advantages. Because of its derivation via the covariant phase space formulation, it is justified to think of it
as a conserved quantity associated with the Hamiltonian. It is defined “in the bulk” and not only at infinity.
It corresponds to the “first-law-integrated” quantities. It has a simple form that matches expressions for the
KBL superpotential but is exact even away from infinity. It is closely related to the stress–energy tensor.
Finally, it is closely related to the first-law area relation at the horizon.
This conserved quantity is equal to the difference in the Komar integrals associated with a given Killing
vector in the background and full spacetimes, plus a non-Komar term. I showed how, when the conserved
charge is a stationarity Killing vector (plus some azimuthal symmetry Killing vectors), the Komar integral
associated with the background spacetime is (up to a multiplicative constant) equal to Λ times the canonical
black hole volume Vξ,B, explaining the black hole volume’s natural appearance in the Smarr relation paired
with Λ (or ˜Λ).
In Chapter 5, having previously shown that the geometric volume which appears in CGKP is the same as
the canonical black hole vector volume Vξ,B, I also showed how to interpret this as the Euclidean volume of
the ellipsoid represented by the spatial part of the black hole region in the pseudo-Cartesian coordinates for
the background AdS metric. I showed how the canonical volume appears naturally in the Euclidean approach
to defining conserved charges. I showed how the method from [87, 34] of defining a Killing potential leads to
the natural emergence of the vector volume. I explained the Vξ,B = r+A/(D −1) relationship by making use
of the PCKY tensor h, the equivalence of the volumes associated with ξ and β, the Gauss–Stokes theorem,
and the fact that the null generator of the horizon is an eigenvector of the PCKY tensor with eigenvalue r+.
In Chapter 6, by making use of the “infinitesimal coordinate transformation” method I demonstrated in
four dimensions how, when l is kept constant but the angular parameter a varies, the variation of integral
of I ¯ζ is equal to κδA/8π in a set of coordinates where the AdS background metric does not vary, and how
the difference between δI ¯ζ and κδA/8π can be expressed in terms of the Lie derivative associated with the
coordinate transformation resulting from the variation in a. I showed too how in vacuum, this Lie derivative
term vanishes, demonstrating the suitability of
H
H I ¯ζ as a quantity which, when varied, gives κδA/8π. I
showed that the formula
H
H kEH
¯ζ
[δg; g] = κδA/8π could be used even when the horizon radius changes, using
the Lie derivative method.
I argued that a fundamental reason that the charge F associated with β cannot be used to construct
a first-law relation is not so much that it is based on an asymptotically rotating frame—or, relatedly, that
399


## Page 443


the vector β is asymptotically rotating, rather than asymptotically static—but that its degree of rotation
is proportional to the rotation parameter a (parameters ai in higher dimensions), so that varying a (ai)
causes the β vector to point “in different directions,” with respect to the AdS background. This makes
it impossible to find a set of coordinates in which both components of the background metric ¯gab and the
vector βa are unvarying. By contrast there are coordinates where ¯gab and the vectors ξa and ηa (ηa
i in higher
dimensions) are unvarying even as a varies (ai vary). This gives my interpretation for why it is important to
use the asymptotically static Killing vector ξ rather than the principal vector β when constructing conserved
charges. This amounts to a particular explanation for the observation, made elsewhere in the literature, that
the Hamiltonian associated with β is not integrable, but the one associated with ξ is.
In showing how the expression for Iχ simplifies considerably for χ = β, and in demonstrating the
equivalence, up to a numerical constant, of the Komar-difference and non-Komar terms associated with Iβ,
I gave an explanation for the canonical black hole volume appearing naturally when the Smarr relation is
constructed using the quantity F associated with β, rather than in the energy term E associated with ξ. I
found expressions for the conserved charges for an arbitrary µ(r) in order to highlight the simplifications
that occur in vacuum.
7.1
Future Work
To follow up on the Addendum for Chapter 2, Section 2.13, I would like to look more carefully at what
we can say about the vector volume’s dependence on choice of hypersurface in situations where adapted
coordinates do not exist covering the full manifold. For spacetimes where the vector field va vanishes at
some point in the manifold, the statement that the hypersurface must intersect each of the integral curves of
v once does not make sense when talking about the whole manifold rather than the portion of the manifold
covered by a particular adapted coordinate chart. There may be a generalization of that requirement that
does make sense globally.
I find the embedding spacetime introduced in Section 3.4.2 and continuing in Appendix C to be intriguing
and would like to study it further.
In Section 4.6.3, I mentioned that I wanted to expand out δθEH when the background metric is allowed
to vary.
400


## Page 444


The explanation for the equality, up to a numerical constant, of the Komar difference and non-Komar
terms associated β comes down to the observation (6.5.71), resulting from (6.5.70), which are

It is, on the one hand, satisfying to get to this point and to find this equality. On the other hand, it is
unclear whether this is something that “had to happen” or something that “just worked out.” Because the
only Kerr–Schild solution to vacuum Einstein’s equations which are globally asymptotically AdS is Kerr–
AdS, and because the Kerr/Myers–Perry solutions exist as a smooth limit to l →∞so that they too can
essentially be treated the same way, we do not have other spacetimes to compare this to. It could be that the
result (6.5.71) is the consequence of some more fundamental property, or it could be a fortuitous result that
happens only in Kerr–AdS and Kerr/Myers–Perry. It would be interesting to broaden the class of spacetimes
under consideration, particularly to other spacetimes with a PCKY tensor and thus a principal vector, to
see whether there is a more general rule. (There is some discussion of another approach to this in Chapter
8.)
I mentioned some future work in Section 6.4.1 regarding the kEH
χ

seems to hold on a surface of constant t, r under more general circumstances than I considered explicitly in

Throughout this work I mostly omitted the treatment of Kerr–de Sitter, with positive Λ. In fact math-
ematically there are many similarities, but the existence of a cosmological horizon means that most results
having to do with infinite spatial radius are complicated. Some treatments of the asymptotically de Sitter
case which shows the similarity to the Kerr–AdS case are given in [43, 97]. Because Kerr–de Sitter is so
mathematically similar to Kerr–AdS, it also possesses a PCKY tensor and can be written in Kerr–Schild
form, and so it would be interesting to see whether the Iχ forms could be useful in interpreting the Kerr–de
Sitter solutions in the framework given in [43, 97]. I noted already that the relationship between the horizon
area and geometric volume VC = rA/(D −1) observed in these papers can be explained simply in terms of
the PCKY tensor (in Section 5.6). Additionally, the value for the thermodynamic volume associated with

Vc = rcAc

where rc, Ac are the values of the r radius parameter and the area of the cosmological horizon, respectively.
This is the same form as (1.2.19). Because the Kerr–de Sitter solutions and Kerr–AdS solutions are related
by sending l →±il, the arguments given in this thesis—that the geometric volume will appear in the Smarr
relationship if a conserved “energy” F is defined associated with the principal Killing vector β, and the as
a consequence the thermodynamic volume associated with E will have extra terms related to the difference
between ξ and β—will still hold, although in this case ξ is no longer an asymptotically static Killing vector
(since it is not timelike beyond the cosmological horizon), though it remains asymptotically hypersurface-
orthogonal and it continues to have the important property ξ does for the asymptotically AdS black holes,
which is that its direction remains fixed with respect to the de Sitter background (as opposed to β).
In CGKP and future papers (for example, see [4] and references therein), the isoperimetric inequality
is introduced, which states that the ratio of the thermodynamic volume and area always satisfies a specific
inequality. This interesting result is something I would like to examine further.
Even in CGKP’s original paper there are several other solutions which are examined which I have not
addressed here at all. There are of course many other papers dealing with thermodynamic volumes in the
many years since CGKP’s paper came out, a description of which is beyond my scope. Two interesting
examples from CGKP are the rotating pairwise-equal 4-charge black hole in D = 4 gauged supergravity
and the charged rotating black hole in minimal D = 5 gauged supergravity. What is interesting is that
these solutions do not have Kerr–Schild form (with an anti-de Sitter background), and so my approach here
would have to be modified (or even might be inapplicable). I would like to try to apply the BC method of
integrating through solution space for these cases too, so as to get a conserved charge at the horizon. It is
interesting to me, too, that the pattern is still present where an asymptotically static Killing vector must be
used for the first law but a geometric volume term appears when an asymptotically rotating vector is used.
Because, at large distance from the central charges, the solution approaches Kerr–AdS, it might be that
many of the methods used here to discuss Kerr–AdS could be used asymptotically and then “transferred to
the horizon” in some way.
402


## Page 446


Another example of a spacetime with interesting properties are the black ring spacetimes, the thermody-
namics of which are described in [4]. These solutions are vacuum and are rotating, but have a non-spherical
horizon topology. They do not a admit Kerr–Schild decomposition. An exact solution is known for the
D = 5 black ring with Λ = 0 (see [45]) but the method in [4] was to take an approximation of an extremely
thin ring. One interesting feature is that the thermodynamic volume differs by a large amount from the
expected geometric volume. Because the thermodynamic volume can be taken even for the Λ = 0 case by
taking a l →∞limit, it would be interesting to compare in more detail the thermodynamic volume to the
vector volume for the Λ = 0 exact solution.
Another natural next step would be to consider solutions with NUT parameters. These complicate the
topology very considerably, but the advantage of the Kerr–NUT and Kerr–AdS–NUT spacetimes is that
they still possess the PCKY tensor h. A treatment of the Kerr–NUT case appears in [16] and a treatment
of Kerr–AdS–NUT appears in [116]. I argued in Section 3.3.1 that the Kerr–NUT–AdS spacetimes could be
interpreted as the result of a “multiple Kerr–Schild” perturbation to a pure AdS solution, with the significant
caveat that the Kerr–Schild vectors for the NUT parameters would be complex. It might be possible to use
Iχ constructed from the real null vector to account for the difference between Kerr–NUT–AdS and simply
NUT–AdS, or it might be possible to construct a Iχ using the complex null vector to account for the NUT
parameters. I also note that the existence of h means that we might be able to find some sort of equivalent
for the volume–area relationship.
There is interesting work on dynamic black holes, such as [69] (and references therein). For spherically
symmetric dynamic black holes, I already pointed out in Chapter 2 that the black hole volume, introduced
by Hayward, associated with the Kodama vector, is a generalization of the vector volume. There might be
a similar generalization in dynamic black holes away from spherical symmetry.
I touched on the physical process version of the first law of black hole mechanics in Section 6.6. I would
like to examine the stress–energy tensor for a more realistic model of energy and angular momentum flow
into a black hole, perhaps in the presence of an accretion disk.
My general approach was to interpret the thermodynamic volume Vth more by its appearance in the
Smarr relation than by its appearance in the first law. This is because the scaling relation means that its
appearance in the Smarr relation automatically implies its appearance in the first law, given that the first
law is otherwise satisfied with Λ (or ˜Λ) kept constant. This meant my focus was somewhat narrow, in trying
403


## Page 447


to interpret the appearance of Vth in the Smarr relation, rather than in the first law. Along those lines, I
did not spend time in this writing on the physical mechanisms which could give rise to a varying Λ.
My focus has generally also been more on black hole mechanics rather than thermodynamics, so that I
have not discussed, for example, Hawking–Page phase transitions, and only touched on Euclidean methods
in Section 5.4 to show how the volume enters into them. It would be worthwhile to take a closer look at
thermodynamic methods and how my work related to black hole mechanics and the geometric interpretation
of volume fits in. This general field has been dubbed “black hole chemistry,” and is an extremely active area
of research: see [96, 106] for reviews.
In Section 6.6.5, I mentioned that I would like to try to break the Ricci tensor down into its constituent
components to show why the second-order terms drop out when the metric is of the form (6.6.76) but not
of the form (6.6.81).
The work of [28, 60] considering Λ as a type of “electric charge” resulting from a gauge field is intriguing
and may be worth pursuing, particularly in terms of whether there is some way to use the BC method to
construct the Smarr relation in this case.
7.2
Final Words
With the increased number of observations of black holes with the Event Horizon Telescope and via gravita-
tional wave detection of black hole mergers, black holes and their properties are a constant, ongoing subject
of interest. I hope that my analysis of the role that the black hole volume plays in black hole thermodynamics
enriches this developing and fascinating field.
404


## Page 448


Chapter 8
Additional Note
Since submitting the thesis, I noticed the following (not appearing in the thesis proper).
One of the questions addressed in my thesis is why the conserved quantity F associated with the vector β
is equal, up to a simple numerical factor, to the difference between the Komar integrals associated with β in
the full and background spacetime, when this is not true for the asymptotically-static vector ξ. In the thesis
I addressed this by showing that the covariant phase space methods of Wald and the integration through
solution space method of Barnich and Comp`ere could be adapted in Kerr–Schild spacetimes to produce a
conserved quantity which is equal to a Komar difference term plus an extra term, related to the symplectic
potential form Θ, and then showed how this last term is equal (up to a numerical factor) to the Komar
difference term associated with β.
Another, in some ways simpler, explanation is the following.
F can also be defined in terms of the
Ashtekar–Magnon–Das (AMD) conserved charge. Here I will show that the AMD conserved charge associated
with β is proportional to the Komar difference term. First I will rewrite the AMD conserved quantity F in
a simple form, having to do with the canonical orthonormal frame components. (The canonical orthonormal
frame, which I will refer to henceforth as the orthonormal frame, is the one introduced in Section 3.2.) Then
I will show the Komar difference term and show how the PCKY tensor and the Weyl tensor appear naturally
in the Komar difference, in a way that shows the equivalence of the Komar difference term and the AMD
term (up to a multiplicative factor of (D −2)/(D −3)). I then revisit the Smarr relationship explicitly with
this equivalence.

We then have Nˆn, Nˆα ≃O(r0), ¯ξˆn ≃O(r1), ¯ξˆα ≃O(r−1). The dominant contribution then will be from
Nˆα ¯ξˆn which will be O(r). Meanwhile, √Qα ≃O(r−1), Cnˆnαˆα ≃O(r−D) from (G.1.30) and dS ≃O(rD−2),
so that this term overall is O(r−1), and so vanishes in the large r limit. We are left with
lim
r→∞
I
Cabcdwc ¯ξddSab = 2
l lim
r→∞
I
Cabcdqc ¯ξddSab,
(8.4.17)
so that, given the smallness of ξd −¯ξd compared to ¯ξd itself, confirms (8.4.9) for χa = ξa. The argument for
χa = ηa
i is essentially identical; in this case
∇a(ηi)b = ∂[a(gϕiϕiδϕi
b] )
= ∂[a ln |gϕiϕi(ηi)b]
(8.4.18)
Since, in Kerr–AdS (except for special cases like Schwarzschild–AdS which have even more symmetries), χa
can be constructed as a linear combination of ξa and ηa
i , we conclude that (8.4.9) holds for all χa. And so
we have the interesting result that Peng’s charge is closely connected with the AMD conserved charge.
415


## Page 459


8.5
Additional Note Conclusion
In this additional note chapter, I showed that the particular properties of β related to the Principal Conformal
Killing–Yano tensor h lead to the Komar difference term being (D −3)/(D −2) times the conformal charge
F associated with β. In doing so, the following properties of β and of the spacetime were were used:
• That β points in the direction of the orthonormal frame unit vector eˆn, for large r, according to
β ≃irl−2eˆn, with all other frame components βA vanishing;
• That the combination ∇aβb −∇aβb can be written as the contraction of the Weyl tensor and the
PCKY tensor h;
• That h can be decomposed into ren ∧eˆn (as well as other terms which do not end up contributing to
the large-r calculation for the Komar difference term);
• That Ω= r−1 can be used as the conformal factor for defining the AMD charge;
• That the Weyl tensor components CAnBn are zero unless A = B, and, similarly, that CµˆµnB is zero
unless B = ˆn.
These all come down to the central role that the orthonormal frame has in describing these spacetimes
as well as how closely h and β are tied up with the structure of the spacetime. This further justifies the
appearance of β in the Smarr relation in which the volume comes out naturally.
As a final note, recall that, from the thesis, ∇aβb = l−2hab. The reader uncomfortable with the compar-
ison between the full spacetime and the background spacetime could instead replace ∇aβb with l−2hab in
the above analysis and the results would be unaffected.
In Appendix G, I calculate the frame components and also evaluate the integral (8.1.15).

## Page 475


Appendix B
Explicit Calculations for Constancy of Komar
Difference Term for Principal and Azimuthal
Vectors for Arbitrary Radius in Vacuum
In this appendix chapter, I revisit the Komar difference terms associated with β (Section B.1) and ηi (Section
B.2) in the Kerr–AdS vacuum. In the main text, I appeal to the vanishing of Ra
b −Ra
b to show that these
terms must be constant. In this chapter I take an alternate approach, calculating the Komar integral at
arbitrary r in both cases (of course, recovering the constancy). The impetus for doing this was to illuminate
what it is about the structure of the quantity being integrated gives rise to this constancy. The arguments
contained herein are interesting, if complicated, so they are included here in the appendix.
Additionally, integrals similar to those in this chapter appear in the calculation of the horizon area in
Section 5.3, itself moved to Appendix A.

B.1.2
Odd-Dimensional Calculation
In odd dimensions, the argument proceeds along different lines. Let the right-hand side of (B.1.6) be given by
f(y1, . . . , yn−1). f is completely antisymmetric in the yα. The integration limits are such that f is evaluated
between yα = aα+1 and aα. We can expand this out as
h
[f(y1, . . . , yn−1)]y1=a1

= f(a1, a2, . . . , an−2, an−1) −f(a1, a2, . . . , an−2, an) + . . . .
(B.1.10)
There are, before cancellations, 2n−1 terms in the expansion, since there are two choices (upper and lower
limit) for each variable yα in the set. Because of the antisymmetry of f, however, all f terms vanish if two of
the entries are equal. Since aα+1 is the lower limit for yα and the upper limit for yα+1, any terms where the
lower limit is chosen for yα and the upper limit is chosen for yα+1 will lead to f being zero. This means that
if the lower limit is taken for yα, the lower limit must also be taken for yα+1 in order to have a nonvanishing
term.
This means that only terms which survive will be the ones for which the upper limit is taken for the first
435

appear. Since the lower limit is chosen n −i times, this term will be accompanied by a sign of (−1)n−i. Let
f(ˆai) be equal to the value of f in this case—where ai is omitted:

B.3.1
Monomial Expansion of ˜P
It will be useful to expand ˜P in terms of a sum of monomials. In what follows we treat the quantities yα as
generic and do not require, for the moment, that they obey the implicit ordering y1 > y2 > . . . > yn−1.
Let c1, . . . , cn−1 be nonnegative integers and let Bc1...cn−1 be an associated coefficient. Since ˜P consists
of terms where yα appear entirely in the numerator, and only appear in squares, ˜P will necessarily be a sum
of monomials which consist of products of nonnegative even powers of yα. Thus we can write it as

(B.3.6)
This means that coefficients B are antisymmetric under interchange of the values in the first and second

Bccc3...cn−1 = −Bccc3...cn−1, which can only be the case of Bccc3...cn−1 vanishes:

(B.3.7)
This followed from ˜P being antisymmetric under interchange of y1 and y2. Since ˜P is antisymmetric under
any interchange of yα, yβ for α ̸= β, we can conclude through the same line of argument that Bc1...cn−1 is
antisymmetric under exchange of any two of its index values and that the coefficient Bc1...cn−1 where any of
the ci are equal to each other must be zero.

There are (n −1)(n −2)/2 binomials in the product, and so when expanding this out, before cancellation,

an even number of time. Of all the 2(n−1)(n−2)/2 monomials in the expansion (before cancellation), only one

Appendix C
Embedding
This follows from Section 3.4.2. This appendix is expanded from the version of the appendix appearing in
the thesis.
We will explicitly write out the metric for the “embedding” spacetime. This is the spacetime of dimension
(D + 1) which is associated with the D-dimensional GKAdS spacetime in a natural way.
The metric satisfies
ds2 = d¯s2 + 2µ(r)

U and ka are exactly the expressions (3.4.19) and (3.4.8) (respectively), where r is defined implicitly by
(3.4.5). The condition that ka is null is simply the same as the definition of r, so ka is automatically null
and so the metric is of Kerr–Schild form.
If the constraint (3.4.2) is applied to this spacetime we recover GKAdS.
I will now specify to the case µ(r) = m, so that it is the metric associated with the embedding of Kerr–
AdS. I will show that this metric is Ricci-flat (Rab = 0). To do so I will introduce alternate coordinates

In the form (C.0.12), it becomes clear that (D + 1)-dimensional embedding spacetime is a Brinkmann
warped product spacetime of the form introduced by [17], and examined in detail, for instance, by Ortaggio
et al. [110]. (I did not mention this connection explicitly in the thesis.) Following the latter, if ds2 is the
metric for an n-dimensional Einstein space with R = 2nΛ/(n −2) which can be written in the form
ds2 = dz2
f(z) + f(z)d˜s2
(C.0.13)
with
f(z) = −λz2 + (2d)z + b,
λ =
Λ
(n −1)(n −2)
(C.0.14)
and where d˜s2 is the metric for an (n −1)-dimensional space, then the (n −1)-dimensional space is also an
Einstein space with ˜R = (n −1)(n −2)(λb + d2). The particular case λ = b = 0 then is
ds2 =
dz2
(2d)z + (2d)zd˜s2,
(C.0.15)
where I’ve inserted the parentheses around the constant d to distinguish (d)z from dz.
Taking y =
455


## Page 499


R
dz/
p
(d)z = 2
p
z/d,
ds2 = dy2 + (d2)y2d˜s2.
(C.0.16)
In this case the metric associated with dy2 is Ricci-flat and the one associated with d˜s2 has ˜R = d2(n −
1)(n −2). This is almost the same as the form (C.0.12), except for the leading negative sign. In a footnote,
Ortaggio et al. also point out that they could include a timelike z by performing a Wick rotation, which is
even closer to the form I have here, but they still assume that the full spacetime with ds2 is Lorentzian, so
that d˜s2 would be Euclidean in signature. Indeed, taking y = iL, d = i/l and applying to (C.0.16) gives
ds2 = −dL2 + L2
l2 d˜s2,
(C.0.17)
reproducing (C.0.12).
As pointed out by Ortaggio, the results of Brinkmann are essentially signature-
independent, and so indeed we expect the (D + 1)-dimensional spacetime given by (C.0.12) to be Ricci-flat.
Embedding Kerr–de Sitter solutions into Ricci-flat spacetimes according to something like (C.0.16) has
been performed in examining black strings, as in [133] (for the Schwarzschild-de Sitter case), and Ortaggio
include an embedding of Kerr–AdS black holes into a negative-curvature spacetime of dimension one higher
in [110].
Embeddings having to do with Kerr–NUT–AdS were examined in [92], which includes several
useful proofs, including the relationship between the existence of a closed conformal Killing–Yano tensor in
the “seed” metric and in the embedding metric. (I will elaborate on this point in a few paragraphs.) I have
not encountered the particular embedding I describe here summarized by (C.0.12), as applied to Kerr–AdS
specifically embedded into a Ricci–flat spacetime (as opposed to Kerr–dS) in the literature. One possible
reason this (to my knowledge) has not been examined in the literature is suggested by [136], who state,
regarding that the case where an Einstein space with negative curvature is embedded into Ricci-flat space
of dimension one higher, “Real embeddings exist, but may involve bulk geometries with multiple timelike
directions. Not knowing of any physical interpretations of spacetimes with multiple timelike directions, we
regard this class of embeddings as containing some physical illness, however if Wick rotations are taken into
account, such embeddings may still yield interesting results.”
The point I make here is that this “embedding spacetime” should not be a big surprise, and the existence of
two timelike dimensions is one reason it may not have been examined before. It is nevertheless interesting that
456


## Page 500


the embedding spacetime has such an attractive form, (C.0.1), in terms of the pseudo-Cartesian coordinates.
While the vanishing of the Ricci tensor for the (D + 1)-dimensional metric follows directly from the
Brinkmann warped product argument, I perform the calculation via the Christoffel symbols, following a
similar calculation to many papers such as [117, 19], in Appendix C.1. It is also noteworthy that ka is
not only null, but also an affinely parametrized geodesic, which I demonstrate in Section C.2. This is not
surprising: it was pointed out by [110] that a WAND in the seed spacetime is also a WAND in the full
spacetime, and that [105] considers Brinkmann warped products of Kerr–Schild spacetimes, pointing out
that the results are still generalized Kerr–Schild spacetimes.
An open question is whether this embedding spacetime, which has a very natural-seeming form and
is Ricci flat, can be used to gain some deeper insight about the Kerr–AdS spacetimes. One first step is to
consider the equivalent of the PCKY tensor. As stated, Krtouˇs et al. [92] consider warped products involving
the Kerr–NUT–AdS class of solutions. Their general setting involves a seed spacetimes of dimensions ˜D and
¯D, with respective metrics ˜g and ¯g, along with “warp factor” ˜w, to produce a full ( ˜D + ¯D)-dimensional
spacetime with metric
g = ˜g + ˜w2g.
(C.0.18)
In our case here, ˜g = −dL2, ˜w = L/l and g is the metric for Kerr–AdS. One of the results in [92] is that if
there exists a closed conformal Killing–Yano tensor q-form ¯h associated with ˜g, then there will be a closed
CKY tensor associated with the full metric g given by
h = ˜wq+1˜ϵ ∧¯h,
(C.0.19)
where ˜ϵ is the Levi-Civita tensor associated with ˜g. Applied to our case here, using the PCKY tensor 2-form,
we expect that the embedding spacetime will have a closed CKY tensor 3-form h3
h3 = L3
l3 dL ∧h2
(C.0.20)
where h2 is the PCKY tensor for the D-dimensional spacetime. This can be written using the form (E.8.22)

C.2
Direct Calculation of Geodesicity of Null Vector
As an additional interesting observation, we can show that ka is geodesic in the flat (D + 1)-dimensional
spacetime. We could do so by appealing to the Christoffel symbols (and showing that if ka is geodesic
in the D-dimensional spacetime, it follows that it will be geodesic in the (D + 1)-dimensional spacetime).
Another approach is to proceed directly, using the fact that the background spacetime is flat, so that, in the
pseudo-Cartesian coordinates, the covariant derivative with respect to the background spacetime is just the
partial derivative.

E.8
Calculations Related to PCKY Tensor
E.8.1
PCKY Vector Exterior Derivative in Terms of Curvature Tensors and PCKY Tensor
∇aβb is written in terms of h and the Ricci and Riemann tensors in various places in the literature (see, for
instance, [48]). I show a derivation for interest, then rewrite the result in terms of the Weyl tensor and make
some additional comments.
We can write, using the definition of β, the CKY condition on h, and the relationship between the second

The results so far (except the ones that used the explicit values for the curvature forms) apply to a generic
CKY tensor (which does not need to be closed) and its associated vector. In the GKNAdS spacetimes, where
β is a Killing vector, we can use the Killing condition, ∇aβb = ∇[aβb] to simplify the expression to (3.2.80).
The fact that ∇aβb can be expanded in terms of the ωµ itself guarantees that k will be an eigenvector of
491


## Page 535


∇aβb. However it is worth teasing this out a little further. We have,
ka∇aβb =
1

We now have three types of terms, the Weyl term, the Ricci tensor terms, and the Ricci scalar term. I will
show that each of these is proportional to kb.
For the Ricci scalar term, k is an eigenvector of hab so that habka ∝kb.
For the Ricci tensor terms, k is also an eigenvector of the Ricci tensor, since k = ˜mn and ˜mn is an
eigenvector of the Ricci tensor as stated in Section 3.2.5. It is also stated in [109, 105] that if ka is geodetic,
it is automatically an eigenvector of the Ricci tensor and a WAND, if the background is Minkowski or anti-de
Sitter. ([105] also shows that vacuum solutions with ∇aka ̸= 0 require that H essentially take the form it
does for Kerr–AdS, though the form it takes in [105] is slightly more general because it includes the case
where some of the eigenvalues yα are degenerate.)
Ricci tensor terms here are proportional to k because k is an eigenvector of both Rab and h: Rc

bkc ∝kb.
For the Weyl term, we note first that Cabcdhcdkakb = 0 by symmetry–antisymmetry of a, b. Next, we will
use the Petrov Type D condition. Following [62], use the convention that Cabcdpaubvcwc = W(p, u, v, w) for
arbitrary vectors p, u, v, w. Using the decomposition of h given by (3.3.13), for an as-yet arbitrary vector v

E.8.3
Raising and Lowering h
We show that if hab has the same coordinate expression in the full and background spacetime, that so does
hab. We have,
gacgbdhcd = (¯gac −Hkakc)(¯gbd −Hkbkd)hcd
= (¯gac −Hkakc)(¯gbdhcd + Hkbkdhdc)
= (¯gac −Hkakc)(¯gbdhcd + Hkb(−rkc))
= ¯gac¯gbdhcd −¯gbdHka(kchcd) −Hrkbka
= ¯gac¯gbdhcd −¯gbdHka(−rkd) −Hrkbka
= ¯gac¯gbdhcd + Hrkakb −Hrkakb
= ¯gac¯gbdhcd.
(E.8.36)
Thus the raised form of h is the same for both metrics so we can write hab without ambiguity. Note however
that there is an ambiguity in the mixed form.

## Page 579


Appendix H
Extended Abstract
This is a longer version of the abstract.
Gibbons et al. [53] and Cvetiˇc et al. [34] consider the first law of black hole thermodynamics as well as
Smarr relations in the Kerr–anti-de Sitter spacetime. Their papers suggest some “open questions,” or at
least partially open questions, some of which have to do with the volume associated with black holes, which
I address by adapting the work of Barnich and Comp`ere [13] to define a (D −2)-form Iχ associated with a
generic Killing vector χ, for which the energy E associated with an asymptotically static Killing vector ξ can
be written as E =
H
Iξ on an arbitrary 2-surface enclosing the black hole. I use this form to address these
“open questions” in the literature.
Given a D-dimensional region R in a D-dimensional spacetime, if there exists a divergence-free vector
field va such that v is tangent to the boundary of R, I define the vector volume associated with v, R to
be Vv,R =
R
Σ vadΣa, where Σ is a hypersurface spanning R and where Σ intersects each of the integral
curves of va lying within R exactly once (at least in some part of the spacetime which can be covered by
coordinates adapted to the vector va). For a finite region, this volume as defined vanishes, but for a region
infinite in extent it can be nonzero. I show that, with certain caveats, this is constant and independent of
choice of such surface Σ. I explore some more of its properties and relate the quantity to black holes. This
definition coincides with one by Parikh [111] and I also show that this volume, applied to a black hole using
the canonically normalized stationarity Killing vector, is equal to the geometric volume of Cvetiˇc et al. [34].
In Gibbons et al. [53], the energy for arbitrary dimensional (D ≥4) Kerr–anti-de Sitter black holes
536


## Page 580


E was found by integrating a first-law variation formula δE = P
i ΩiδJi + TδS, with black hole angular
momenta Ji, angular velocity Ωi, temperature T and entropy S. The cosmological constant ˜Λ satisfying
Rab = ˜Λgab for Ricci tensor Rab, metric gab, was kept fixed. This energy was found to correspond to the
Ashtekar–Magnon–Das (AMD) energy, but only when calculated in an asymptotically static frame, rather
than an asymptotically rotating one. In Cvetiˇc et al.’s paper [34], the first law was extended by interpreting
E as an enthalpy, and interpreting ˜Λ as a thermodynamic variable proportional to a pressure, so that the
first law now reads δE = P
i ΩiδJi + TδS + Θδ˜Λ, where Θ is a thermodynamic potential proportional to the
so-called thermodynamic volume Vth = −16πΘ/(D −2). Due to a scaling symmetry, the above variables
also automatically satisfy the Smarr relation (D −3)E = (D −2) (P
i ΩiJi + TS) −2Θ˜Λ.
If instead a certain asymptotically rotating frame is used, the equivalent AMD “energy” (which I denote by
F), along with angular velocities ωi in the asymptotically rotating frame, satisfies the modified Smarr relation
(D−3)F = (D−2) (P
i ΩiJi + TS)−2Θ′˜Λ, associated with a geometric volume Vgeo = −16πΘ′/(D−2). This
geometric volume, given by
R √−gdD−1x with metric determinant g and product of coordinate differentials
with the time omitted dD−1x and integrated below the horizon down to the singularity, is the quantity
which is equal to my vector volume. In general Vth ̸= Vgeo. [34] also noted the intriguing relation Vgeo =
r+A/(D −1), where r+ is the value of the usual spheroidal (Boyer–Lindquist) r coordinate on the horizon.
These papers (either implicitly or explicitly) raise the following questions: what is it that makes it
necessary to base the conserved energy (or enthalpy) on an asymptotically static frame, rather than an
asymptotically rotating one? Why does the volume appear more naturally in the relation with the asymp-
totically rotating frame rather than the asymptotically static one? Why is the volume proportional to the
black hole area, with proportionality constant equal to r+/(D −1)? I address each of these questions.
Barnich and Comp`ere [13] argued that conserved quantities which satisfy a first law variation could be
found by integrating through solution space, and that these integrals could then be used to construct a Smarr
relation. I show how applying this method to Kerr–Schild spacetimes results in simplifications, leading to
a (D −2)-form Iχ associated with a Killing vector χ. When integrated over a (D −2) surface, this gives a
conserved quantity for solutions to Einstein’s equations. When χ is the asymptotically-static Killing vector,
the result is E, and when χ is an azimuthal symmetry Killing vector, the result is −Ji.
The exterior derivative of Iχ is also shown to be related to the stress–energy tensor of the spacetime,
and with certain caveats, is also shown to satisfy a horizon variation law even when the spacetime is not
537


## Page 581


a solution to Einstein’s equations. The integral of Iχ over a (D −2)-surface is shown to be equal to the
difference between the Komar integrals associated with χ for the full and background spacetimes, plus one
non-Komar term. I show how the Komar term associated with the background metric is equal to the black
hole vector volume. I use Iχ to argue that the reason that the asymptotically rotating Killing vector is a poor
candidate to base a conserved quantity on is less because it is rotating, but because there are no coordinates
in which both it and the Kerr–Schild background metric do not vary when the rotational parameter varies.
I show how the use of the asymptotically rotating vector simplifies the expression for Iχ in such a way that
the vector volume appears naturally in the Smarr relationship in this case. I also show how the volume–area
relation follows directly from the existence of the Principal Conformal Killing–Yano tensor h.
538
